# Ed Lawrence · the movement library

Twelve Ed Lawrence scripts, every line where he moves the viewer forward, sorted by technique. His audience is business owners who want YouTube to bring them clients, so his pain is lost sales and wasted videos and his desire is leads, buyers and money from fewer views. Swap those for ours and every template in here works.

How to use it. Part 1 is how he does it, in plain terms. Part 2 is the library, one section per technique, each opening with what he's doing and a set of templates with blanks, then every example from all twelve videos tagged with the video number. Part 3 is a flow map of each script, beat by beat, so you can see how the devices chain together. Part 4 is the twelve scripts in full.

Sources: the "Ed Lawrence Script Analysis" page in Notion, videos 1 to 12, about 32,000 words.

---

# Part 1 · How Ed keeps a viewer moving

## Most of the script is movement

Go line by line through the twelve scripts and around 85 per cent of lines carry at least one hook, promise, signpost, gap, pain line, result line or callback. The teaching itself is small and simple, a chart, a rule, three steps, and nearly everything wrapped around it is there to tell the viewer where they are, why the next bit matters to them, and what they'll get if they stay. He boils each idea down to something a viewer could repeat after one hearing, and then spends most of his words framing it.

## The fifteen moves he repeats

**1. He names the section before he delivers it, and hangs a payoff on the name.** He almost never says a bare "Step 4". He says "Which brings us on to Step 5, which is going to stop you hitting record and watching the footage back and going 'god, no'", or "step three, which is the bridge from views to leads and sales", or "the boring sign absolutely nobody wants to hear". The label tells you where you are and the clause after it tells you why to stay.

**2. He grounds every section in its first line.** "Step 1 is all about this." "And it's made up of three parts." "This is the viewer cycle, and it's happening on your channel right now." "So a great story is just about…" The viewer always knows what they're inside, which is what lets him move fast.

**3. He chains sections with a problem, so the next step arrives as the answer.** "But how do you get that number in the title nice and big? This is where step two comes in." The end of one point hands the viewer a new problem, and the next section is the fix.

**4. He re-hooks inside sections, constantly, with tiny phrases.** "The thing is", "But worse", "But here's the thing", "But it gets even worse", "here's what most people get wrong", "But the second thing that really matters is", "this is where it gets interesting", "Now do you want to know something crazy?", "So check this out." These do the same job as a signpost at a tenth of the length. They reopen tension right when a point is resolving, which is exactly when a viewer would otherwise drift.

**5. He opens loops and pays them off later.** "You're going to find out why in a minute." "At the end of the video I'm going to show you what this AI is." "Before I break down the three formats that can make you millions, you need to understand…" "Before I give you a bit of a shock as to just how bad…" A promise parked early holds the viewer across several sections, and when he pays it off he says so ("And that brings us on to the AI I showed you at the very start").

**6. He asks the viewer's question out loud, then answers it in two words.** "Would I buy that? Well, no." "Do you have to build a spreadsheet like I did? Well, no." "Should you turn them off too? Well, no. Maybe not. And I'll explain." "But how do you find out how long people have been watching? Well, it's actually really easy." The question makes the viewer lean in and the short answer keeps the pace up.

**7. He voices the viewer's doubt before they can.** "That sounds like utter internet marketing talk." "Now you might be going 'Great, I'm miles off of that.' But check this out." "Now before you think 'Oh, Ed's gone all AI'…" Naming the objection first stops it from quietly pulling the viewer out.

**8. He sets up a misconception and then breaks it.** "Let's start with a common misconception about video quality." "This is what people don't understand about business or YouTube." "Now, we're told this is powerful because… The thing is…" "The truth is, we're all completely wrong, but also right at the same time." The viewer's current belief becomes the open loop.

**9. He keeps every kind of viewer in with conditionals.** "If you're in a position where your ad revenue makes a considerable difference to your life, no, do not turn it off." "If you can't visually show a transformation, don't panic, because this can still work." "If you're like a doctor and you've never had a bad back, that's okay." "Now don't panic if you're not in the top tier. Trust me. You can." Nobody gets a reason to leave.

**10. He ends each point on the result, in the viewer's world.** "And when you do that? You won't turn viewers off." "By the third video, boom, they're on your email list or they're booking a call." "So the ideal clients watch you for longer and turn to buyers faster." The desire comes back every few sentences, always as something that happens in their business.

**11. He makes the stakes concrete with numbers and escalates them.** In the ads video it's $260, then $790, then "the thing is, that's just one video", then $9,310, $27,930, $21,000. In the killer-video script "But it gets even worse… And then it gets even worse." Each rung of the ladder is a re-hook.

**12. He carries one image, number or example through the whole video and calls back to it.** The chocolate drawer and the rice cake. The $240,000 video, which later becomes "actually more like half a million". The four videos referring each other at 70 to 100 views an hour. The homeless-in-Airbnbs story. The same demo channel at the end of every step. Callbacks make separate steps feel like one idea and reward the viewer for staying.

**13. He proves things as he goes, often by doing it in front of them.** He reads his own intro back and points at "this first line". He tells the same lesson two ways and asks "Can you feel the difference?" He turns the light off and on. Proof arrives as a small reveal rather than a claim.

**14. He breaks the pattern with himself.** Self-mockery ("I was a homeless man who couldn't fit anything else in my bag"), asides ("Sorry Greg"), sudden tone drops ("Illusion ruined", "Flipping rice cake"), mock voices of the viewer or a client. These arrive right after a dense bit of teaching.

**15. He closes by opening a new need, never by recapping.** The last beat names the next problem the viewer now has ("once you've shot this, you then need to edit it so you don't put buyers off") and sends them to a video that fixes it, with the result attached. Often with social proof ("they've already commented saying it blew up their channels").

## Rhythm

From the notes on the Notion page. He doesn't pause after "but" or "so". He runs into the line fast and pauses before the reveal, then drops into a more intimate tone as he lands the point. "But the only thing that changed… is the light."

## What this means for our scripts

A section of ours should look like his. One line that names it and hangs a payoff on it. One line that grounds it. The idea, boiled down. And then every few sentences inside it, a small re-hook, a rhetorical question with a short answer, a pain line or a result line in their world, a callback, or a conditional that keeps someone in. The signposts between sections change shape every time. Sometimes they lead with the result, sometimes with the pain the section removes, sometimes with a question, sometimes with a warning, sometimes with a problem the last section left behind.

# Part 2 · The library, by technique

Each technique opens with what Ed is doing and some templates with blanks, written for any audience. Then every example from the twelve videos, tagged [V1] to [V12]. Quotes are exact, including his punctuation. A line can appear under more than one technique when it does more than one job.

Techniques in this part: hook (opening), promise / value statement, signpost / transition, grounding, mid-section re-hook / retention line, curiosity gap / open loop, rhetorical question, misconception / contrarian setup, pain / empathy, desire / result, stakes / cost, conditional bridge, reminder / callback, proof / credibility, story lead-in, pattern interrupt, close / next-video send.

## Hook (opening)  ·  27 examples

He opens in one of four ways. A strange event ("Something weird has happened"), a hard judgement on the viewer ("The second your video loads, people judge you"), a question aimed at a wish they have ("Have you ever dreamt of…"), or a big specific number. Within two or three lines he names the pain, undercuts the obvious fix, drops a proof point, and gets to the promise.

**Templates**

- Something [strange / unexpected] happened that completely changed the way I think about [topic].
- The second you [do the thing], [they judge / decide] you. And if they don't like [it], [loss].
- Have you ever [wanted result], but there's just something stopping you?
- [Most people] struggle to [result] because they're stuck [doing the old thing that doesn't work].
- But that doesn't mean you need to [expensive / hard fix]. And I know this because [proof].

**Every example**

*Video 1 · I Turned OFF My YouTube Ads, Maybe You Should Too*

- "Something weird has happened that's completely changed the way I think about YouTube."
- "And that is — the other day I was looking at my data, and I did some maths… and it occurred to me that YouTube ads that play before my videos could actually be impacting my sales and my business."
- "So I turned them off immediately."

*Video 2 · Copy This YouTube Studio Style, It'll Blow Up Your Business*

- "The second your video loads, people judge you. And if they don't like what they see, they're going to leave. And you're never going to generate views and sales."

## Promise / value statement  ·  77 examples

Every promise has the same bones: what he'll show you, then "so you can" or "so that" and the result in the viewer's business. He also makes small promises mid-video ("let me give you an example", "I'll go into more detail on how to plan these out in a minute") which act as mini open loops.

**Templates**

- So in this video I'm going to show you [the thing], so you can [result in their world].
- I'm going to give you [my simple system] that [proof], so [the people you want] [do the thing you want] faster.
- And all you need to do is [three] very specific things. So let me show you how this works.
- So let me show you behind the scenes of [my own thing] so you can see how this works.
- I'll go into more detail on [part] in a minute.

## Signpost / transition  ·  245 examples

The most frequent device in the library. The plain ones ("Which brings us on to Step 2", "So to start", "The next thing you want to include…") keep the viewer oriented. The strong ones hang a payoff or a warning on the label ("the next step is going to get you a lot of emails", "there's another step we can't ignore", "this is where you need to work out…"). He also signposts with a question he then answers ("So why did I turn YouTube ads off?") and with a problem the last section left open.

**Templates**

- Which brings us on to step [N], which is going to [stop pain / get result].
- So to start, we just need to look at [the misconception / the basics].
- So step [N] is all about [thing].
- Now the next thing you want to [include / do] is going to [result].
- Okay, so after that there's another step we can't ignore.
- [Problem the last step raised]? Well, this is where step [N] comes in.
- All right, so why [did I / should you] [thing]?
- So that's the basics of the problem, which is only scratching the surface. So let me show you the bad news.

**Every example**

*Video 1 · I Turned OFF My YouTube Ads, Maybe You Should Too*

- "All right, so why did I turn YouTube ads off?"
- "Well, let's just start with the basics"
- "So that's the basics of the problem…"
- "So let me show you the bad news."
- "But let's just take this video here that makes me over $3 every view to continue the demo."

## Grounding  ·  130 examples

The first line of a section names it plainly so the viewer knows what they're looking at. "Step 1 is all about this." "And it's made up of three parts." "Act One is your past." He also grounds by pointing at something ("So you see that big old spike? That's link clicks") and by defining a term the moment he uses it ("dollar per view divides…"). Many grounding lines end with a label he'll reuse ("I call this conversion content", "I've named this the premium video system").

**Templates**

- So [step N / this] is all about [thing].
- And it's made up of [three] parts. The first is…, the second is…, and the third is…
- This is [the concept], and it's happening [in your work] right now.
- So a [good X] is just about [one-sentence definition].
- [Term] is basically [plain definition], so you can [what it lets you see].
- I call this [name].

## Mid-section re-hook / retention line  ·  118 examples

The tiny phrases that do a signpost's job inside a section. They reopen tension right when a point is about to resolve. "The thing is", "But worse", "But here's the thing", "But it gets even worse", "this is where it gets interesting", "here's what most people get wrong", "So check this out", "Now do you want to know something crazy?", "This is probably my favourite thing about…", "Step one is absolutely critical, because if you get this wrong nothing else will work." He also re-hooks with a direct keep-watching line when a section feels complete ("continue to watch the video, because now I'm going to give you…").

**Templates**

- The thing is, [complication].
- But worse, [bigger consequence].
- But it gets even worse.
- But here's the thing.
- But here's what most people get wrong.
- And this is where it gets interesting.
- So check this out.
- Now do you want to know something crazy?
- This part is critical, because if you get this wrong, [nothing else works / result is lost].
- And if you think you need [to fix this], stay with me, because now I'm going to give you [the simplest way to do it].

**Every example**

*Video 1 · I Turned OFF My YouTube Ads, Maybe You Should Too*

- "So that's the basics of the problem… which is kind of only scratching the surface of the damage it might be doing. So let me show you the bad news."
- "The thing is — that's just one video."
- "But I guess the question is… should you turn them off too?"

*Video 2 · Copy This YouTube Studio Style, It'll Blow Up Your Business*

## Curiosity gap / open loop  ·  125 examples

A promise parked for later, or a line that withholds the answer. "You're going to find out why in a minute." "At the end of the video I'm going to show you what this is." "Before I break down [the payoff], you need to understand…" He often teases with a quality word ("the first sign is kind of weird", "a trap you're almost guaranteed to fall into") and names the payoff when he closes the loop.

**Templates**

- And you're going to find out why in a minute.
- At the end of the video I'm going to show you [the thing I just teased].
- But before we get on to [the payoff], you first need to [work out / understand] [prerequisite].
- Before I give you a bit of a shock as to just how bad [thing] could be, let's start with the basics.
- Now, you have to avoid the trap you're almost guaranteed to fall into.
- And that brings us on to the [thing] I showed you at the very start.

## Rhetorical question  ·  117 examples

Two kinds. The question the viewer is thinking, answered in two words ("Would I buy that? Well, no."), and the question that puts the viewer in a scene ("Have you ever been invited to someone's house and…?", "Which of these was shot on a $5,000 camera?"). He also stacks options as a question ("Someone calm and collected? Someone who radiates confidence? Or some guy dancing?") and ends thoughts with "right?" to get a nod.

**Templates**

- But how do you [the obvious next question]? Well, it's actually really easy.
- So should you [do the thing too]? Well, no. Maybe not. And I'll explain.
- Do you have to [do the hard version I did]? Well, no.
- Have you ever [relatable scene]? That's the same feeling [your audience] gets when [the problem].
- So which of these [two examples] [is the good one]?
- So where do you start if you don't want to [pain]?
- Which makes sense, right?

## Misconception / contrarian setup  ·  120 examples

He makes the viewer's current belief the open loop. "Let's start with a common misconception about…" "We're told this is powerful because… The thing is…" "This is what people don't understand about…" "Believe it or not, your hottest fans are not on YouTube." He also contradicts himself on purpose ("Well, no. Maybe not."), which makes the next section feel honest and earned.

**Templates**

- So to start, we need to look at a common misconception about [topic].
- One person's going to tell you [A]. Another is going to say [B]. The truth is, [the useful answer].
- Now, we're told [common advice] because [reason]. The thing is, [what the data / experience shows].
- This is what people don't understand about [topic].
- Believe it or not, [counter-intuitive fact].
- But this doesn't mean you have to [expensive / hard version].

The result comes back every few sentences, always as something that happens in the viewer's business, and often as the last clause of a point. "And when you do that? You won't turn viewers off." "By the third video, boom, they're on your list." It's always specific (a number, a behaviour, a moment), never a feeling on its own.

**Templates**

- The thing is, that's just [one example]. So if we look at [the whole picture]…
- But it gets even worse.
- So [you get] no [result], no [result], and [the whole thing] kind of fails.
- It's the difference between [a thing that works] and one that dies on the runway.

## Conditional bridge  ·  150 examples

"If you…" lines that keep every kind of viewer inside the video. He splits the audience on the spot and gives each group permission to stay ("if you're in a position where…, no, do not turn it off. But if you're in a position where…"). He also uses "if you do that, then [result]" to tie a step to its payoff, and "don't panic if…" to hold anyone who feels behind.

**Templates**

- If you're in a position where [situation A], [do this]. But if you're in a position where [situation B], [do that].
- If you can't [do the ideal thing], don't panic, because this can still work.
- Now if you're [the exception], that's okay. [Why it still applies].
- If you do that, you'll [result].
- So long as you [condition], [result].

## Reminder / callback  ·  84 examples

He refers back to a number, story or image from earlier, so the steps feel like one idea. "Which is, remember, the lower estimate." "Because of what I told you." "What I did when I was homeless and lived in Airbnbs." "So now you know why story matters, the foundations, and how to plan one." That last type is a progress marker: here's what you've got so far, here's what's next.

**Templates**

- [Fact], which, remember, is [the thing from earlier].
- Because of what I told you at the start, [consequence].
- So now you know [A], [B] and [C]. [What's left].
- Just like [the image / story from earlier], [this step].
- And that's what step [N] is all about.

## Proof / credibility  ·  137 examples

Specific numbers, named people, his own data on screen, and demonstrations done in front of the viewer. Proof often arrives inside a promise ("I've made millions from my videos using just this battered old tiny light") or as a before-and-after with a question attached ("What did I do differently?").

**Templates**

- And I know this because [specific result with a number].
- So let me show you [my own data / behind the scenes].
- Here's [before]. And here's [after]. What did I do differently?
- [Named person] did [thing] and [result].
- Can you feel the difference?

## Story lead-in  ·  84 examples

Short stories that start with a situation and a problem, then a turn. "So about 4 to 5 years ago, I had a channel…" "For 18 months, I lived in Airbnbs…" "Now, at this stage I had a bit of a moral dilemma." The story nearly always serves as proof or permission for the step it sits in.

**Templates**

- So about [time] ago, [situation].
- Now, at this stage I had a bit of a [dilemma / problem].
- I once [small, vivid mistake]. [Why it matters now].
- So I was smug. I was like, 'This is going to be easy.' But [what went wrong].

## Pattern interrupt  ·  112 examples

Self-mockery, asides, sudden tone drops, mock voices, a demonstration that breaks the frame. They arrive just after dense teaching. "Would I buy that? Well, no. I only had that because I was a homeless man…" "Illusion ruined." "Sorry Greg." "Flipping rice cake." For our voice these become dry asides and quick self-deprecation rather than swearing or slapstick.

**Templates**

- [Quick self-deprecating aside about himself].
- Wait, let me show you [the thing] for a second.
- [Mock voice of the viewer / client / boss], '[their exact reaction]'.
- [One-word verdict].

## Close / next-video send  ·  14 examples

No recap. He names the next problem the viewer now has and sends them to a video that fixes it, with the result attached, sometimes with social proof. "The thing is, once you've shot this, you then need to edit it so you don't do anything that puts buyers off. So watch this video next, where I'm going to show you…"

**Templates**

- The thing is, once you've [done this], you then need to [next need], so you don't [pain].
- So watch this video next, where I'm going to show you [thing], so you can [result].
- And from the people who've already seen it, [social proof].

# Part 3 · Flow maps, script by script

Each script as a run of beats, labelled by device, so you can see how the moves chain together. Then a short note on what keeps that script moving.

## Video 1 · I Turned OFF My YouTube Ads, Maybe You Should Too

1. [Hook / pattern interrupt] "Something weird has happened that's completely changed the way I think about YouTube."
2. [Story lead-in + curiosity gap] "the other day I was looking at my data, and I did some maths… and it occurred to me that YouTube ads that play before my videos could actually be impacting my sales and my business."
3. [Pattern interrupt / decisive action] "So I turned them off immediately."
4. [Promise + stakes] "I'm going to show you why I think leaving YouTube ads on your videos could be costing you money — and I'm going to help you work out if you should turn them off too."
5. [Rhetorical question / signpost] "All right, so why did I turn YouTube ads off?"
6. [Grounding + open loop] "let's just start with the basics before I give you a bit of a shock as to just how bad leaving ads on your videos could be."
7. [Analogy + rhetorical question] "imagine if every Coke can had a Pepsi logo on the side. That would be absolutely insane, right?"
8. [Pain] "Your competition can potentially appear at the start of your video, trying to get viewers to watch their content, click on their offer, and buy their thing."
9. [Re-hook / escalation] "which is kind of only scratching the surface of the damage it might be doing. So let me show you the bad news."
10. [Proof / credibility] "in the first 5 months of 2025, I generated over $2 million from the traffic YouTube drives to my offer"
11. [Grounding] "it's called dollar per view."
12. [Reminder + stakes] "because of what I told you — I decided to look at what percentage of my viewers they could be driving away"
13. [Stakes escalation] "I've lost $260 in sales… I'm down $790… The thing is — that's just one video."
14. [Stakes climax] "The overall opportunity cost to my business could be up to $21,000 in just the first 5 months of the year."
15. [Rhetorical question / pivot to viewer] "But I guess the question is… should you turn them off too?"
16. [Misconception / contrarian] "Well — no. Maybe not. And I'll explain."
17. [Story lead-in] "So about 4 to 5 years ago, I had a channel that got way more views than this one you're watching."
18. [Conditional bridge] "So if you're in a position where your ad revenue makes a considerable difference to your life — no, do not turn it off. But if you're in a position where you have a business like mine…"
19. [Reminder] "which is — remember — the lower estimate for the amount of people that will click on the ads before it."
20. [Result] "it's going to reveal the potential cost of ads to your business. You can then make the decision from there."
21. [Close / next-video send + desire] "if you want to get your dollar per view up — so you can earn way more money from less views — then watch this video next"

**What keeps it moving.** This script runs almost entirely on escalating stakes. Ed drops one open loop up front ("before I give you a bit of a shock") and then pays it off with a ladder of numbers that gets bigger each rung ($260, $790, $9,310, $27,930, $21,000), with "The thing is — that's just one video" as the bridge that resets the escalation. He grounds every new concept (dollar per view, the 0.5-1.5% click rate) the moment he introduces it, then calls back to it later with "remember" and "because of what I told you" so the maths feels earned. The midpoint pivot is a rhetorical question aimed at the viewer ("should you turn them off too?") answered with a contrarian "Well — no. Maybe not." that opens a second act built on a personal story and a pair of conditional bridges ("if you're in a position where…"). The close reframes the whole video as a stepping stone to a desire ("earn way more money from less views") and sends to the next video.

## Video 2 · Copy This YouTube Studio Style, It'll Blow Up Your Business

1. [Hook / pain] "The second your video loads, people judge you. And if they don't like what they see, they're going to leave."
2. [Stakes] "And you're never going to generate views and sales."
3. [Contrarian + proof] "that doesn't mean you need to spend a ton of cash on production kit… I've made millions from my videos using just this battered old tiny light."
4. [Promise + desire] "I'm going to give you my really simple production system… so the ideal clients watch you for longer and turn to buyers faster."
5. [Signpost + misconception] "so to start, we just need to look at the common misconception about video quality."
6. [Contrarian reveal] "The truth is, we're all completely wrong — but also right at the same time."
7. [Grounding] "you're going to use this chart. So what you can see here are four pillars."
8. [Result] "And when you do that? You won't turn viewers off."
9. [Proof / example] "So this is Chris Do. He's a multimillionaire."
10. [Contrast / pain] "But this is Steve's chart… if he keeps his production quality low, he just stands no chance."
11. [Pain + stakes] "most people start with a chart that looks like this… It could take ages. 50 videos, maybe."
12. [Re-hook] "continue to watch the video — because now I'm going to give you the world's simplest system to level up this column fast."
13. [Signpost / grounding] "All right. So Step 1 is all about this."
14. [Demonstration] "I'm in the same environment — yet the vibe and quality looks so different, right? And the only thing that has changed is the light."
15. [Story / proof] "For 18 months, I lived in Airbnbs and I was traveling the world."
16. [Signpost] "Which brings us on to Step 2."
17. [Rhetorical question / pattern interrupt] "So which of these videos on screen right now was shot on a $5,000 camera — and which one was shot on a 10-year-old camera that once had an entire pint of beer spilled on it…?"
18. [Open loop] "you have a camera that can create like a bit of a blurred background. And you're going to find out why in a minute."
19. [Signpost] "Which brings us on to Step 3."
20. [Rhetorical question / analogy] "So have you ever been invited to someone's house — and walked in and gone, 'Oh my gosh… this person lives in an absolute pigsty.'"
21. [Story / rules] "Rule number one was to not try and do anything fancy" / "rule number two"
22. [Pattern interrupt] "And right now — believe it or not — one sec let me show you... This is my kitchen sink."
23. [Loop close + proof] "And this is why I think a camera that allows you to create this kind of blur effect behind you is important"
24. [Signpost] "Which brings us on to Step 4."
25. [Signpost + pain] "Which brings us on to Step 5 — which is going to stop you hitting record and watching the footage back and going: 'Fucking god, no.'"
26. [Re-hook] "Now this next step is optional — but hang on, let me show you the difference it makes."
27. [Pattern interrupt] "wait, I'll turn it back on"
28. [Close / next-video send] "once you've shot this, you then need to edit it… So watch this video next"

**What keeps it moving.** This script uses "Which brings us on to Step N" as a metronome, but Ed never lets the step label do the work alone, and each step opens with a sensory or rhetorical device (a before/after on screen, a "which camera was this?" quiz, the "pigsty" house question) before any instruction lands. The pre-steps section is a deliberate misconception-then-framework setup ("common misconception" → "we're all completely wrong" → the four-pillar chart) that ends with an explicit retention line ("continue to watch the video — because now I'm going to give you…"). He plants one open loop across steps ("you're going to find out why in a minute" about blurred backgrounds) and closes it in Step 3. The "homeless man in Airbnbs" story is the credibility spine that reappears in Steps 1, 3 and 4 as both proof and permission to be cheap. Pattern interrupts are frequent and self-deprecating ("Illusion ruined", "wait, I'll turn it back on"), and the optional final step is sold with "hang on, let me show you the difference" rather than skipped.

## Video 3 · How To Build A Profitable YouTube Channel In 30 Days

1. [Hook / pain] "Most businesses struggle to generate consistent sales and leads from YouTube because they are stuck using outdated, complex, and time-consuming methods that don't really work."
2. [Promise + proof + result] "I'm going to show you the six-step process responsible for millions in sales for myself, my clients, and how to set it up to flip even a small channel's profitability in just 30 days."
3. [Signpost / grounding] "Step 1 — You need to understand the viewer cycle"
4. [Curiosity gap] "this chart here, which is going to change the way you think about growing a business on YouTube."
5. [Empathy / relatable behaviour] "think about what happens when you find a new creator who you really like. You binge five or six of her videos…"
6. [Grounding + stakes] "This is the viewer cycle, and it's happening on your channel right now."
7. [Desire / contrast] "if you want to build a profitable channel—not just a popular one"
8. [Rhetorical question] "But how do you find out how long people have been watching?"
9. [Signpost + stakes] "Now we move on to Step Two, which is the foundation you have to master—otherwise you can't convert viewers to buyers."
10. [Objection pre-empt] "Now, that might sound like a lot, but it's actually pretty simple if you think of it like this"
11. [Analogy / grounding] "you've got to make sure your YouTube channel looks like this pie."
12. [Stakes] "But worse: if your videos start solving problems your hottest viewers don't care about anymore, they stop clicking altogether."
13. [Open loop] "if you follow the next few steps I'm going to show you too."
14. [Second half of step] "But the second thing that really matters is: … your offer is a pie too"
15. [Send-off tease] "And I'm going to link a video at the end that's going to show you how to do this in way more detail."
16. [Signpost / grounding] "So Step 3 is to set up a flywheel… And it's made up of three parts."
17. [Result] "by the third video — boom — they're on your email list or they're booking a call"
18. [Signpost] "Step 4 is to increase the number of people who enter the flywheel."
19. [Grounding + desire] "The first one, click-through rate… We want to increase that — ideally to 8, 10, or even 12%"
20. [Stakes] "it's the difference between a flywheel that works and one that dies on the runway."
21. [Signpost + contrarian] "Step 5 is to use your pitch video to build an email list — not just drive direct sales. Because here's the truth: not everyone is going to buy right away."
22. [Misconception] "But here's what most people get wrong: they treat the lead magnet as a side thing."
23. [Signpost] "Step 6 is to optimize your flywheel using actual buyer feedback."
24. [Payoff line] "But the answer to that question is gold."
25. [Result / summary] "This is how you go from just making content… to making content that sells."
26. [Close / next-video send] "if you want me to break down the exact pitch video structure I use… just click right here."

**What keeps it moving.** This is the most linear of the four, with every step named up front ("Step 3 is to set up a flywheel") and then immediately grounded ("And it's made up of three parts") before any detail, so the viewer always knows what they are inside. Instead of overt "keep watching" lines, Ed keeps movement with "But worse:", "But the second thing that really matters is:", "here's the truth:" and "here's what most people get wrong:", each of which reopens tension just as a sub-point was resolving. Nearly every step ends on a money-or-clients payoff ("converting cold traffic into warm leads", "way more sales over time", "making content that sells"), and stakes are set as the flip side of the same result ("no views, no sales—and kind of fails", "dies on the runway"). Analogies (the viewer cycle chart, the apple pie) serve as both grounding and empathy, letting him name the viewer's failure without blaming them ("not because of you, but because life moves on"). He tees up the next video twice, once mid-script and once in the close.

## Video 4 · The EXACT Storytelling System That Turns Viewers In To Buyers

1. [Hook / rhetorical question + pain] "Why is it that most people struggle to make money from YouTube videos even though they do everything you're taught… whilst other people like Dan Co and Brian Johnson and myself can generate millions from the platform?"
2. [Curiosity gap / answer tease] "it's because they ignore the most powerful tool on the planet for views and sales and leads — where all you need to do is just tell three different types of stories"
3. [Promise + proof] "I'm going to give you the exact storytelling system… I used to use it to sell out a $5,000 program every time — with no sales calls, no email nurture, no ads, no webinar, no real funnel at all"
4. [Signpost / grounding] "for this storytelling system to work you have to understand first why… Telling stories is so powerful."
5. [Pattern interrupt / challenge] "deliver the same information now in two different ways and then you can make your own mind up about if you bother watching the rest of the video based on it."
6. [Story lead-in + callback] "I've used this first story before in a video — you might have heard it. If you have, just stick with it and I'll explain all the power behind it later."
7. [Story: Option 1] "I discovered this YouTube tack about 20 years ago when I had a lawn care business"
8. [Signpost] "All right, so that was option number one. Now option number two"
9. [Rhetorical question / payoff] "Can you feel the difference between the two?"
10. [Grounding / principle] "people don't remember what you said — they remember how you made them feel."
11. [Re-hook] "But that's not all telling stories is going to do for your sales and your business and your channel. There's a few more reasons"
12. [Proof stories] "In this video, I told a story about how I'd gotten really ill" / "this video from a few years ago… deleted all of my old videos" / "niching down and quitting my old channel"
13. [Signpost / pattern interrupt] "Copy What Works — This is probably my favorite thing about stories."
14. [Rhetorical question / analogy] "'You can have a Rolex, or you can have a fake Rolex — which one do you want?'"
15. [Summary + transition] "so now you know why you must not sleep on them… Let me show you how to easily get into storytelling without it taking up all your time."
16. [Misconception] "what most people think is storytelling is all about making a video like this — with a big epic story from start to finish"
17. [Open loop / signpost] "before I break down the three formats that can make you millions… you need to understand the things… that you have to include"
18. [Contrarian] "people always think telling a story is what's powerful. It's not. Telling a good one is."
19. [Grounding] "the best way to explain… is like this circle. You have the beginning at the top here, and you have the end down here."
20. [Callback to own story] "So my story about the drive started with the problem…"
21. [Sub-foundations] "The next thing that makes a story really flipping powerful" / "So the first works like this" / "Now the next thing" / "And the final thing from the foundations — that's critical you do not blow"
22. [Callback] "remember what I said about how you don't want to be a fake Rolex?"
23. [Summary + signpost] "So really, your circle just looks like this… That's it. That's not too hard, right?"
24. [Warning] "And here's one really important mistake you need to avoid"
25. [Summary + promise] "So now you know why story matters, the foundations, and how to plan one. I'm going to give you the three frameworks…"
26. [Grounding / open loop] "Act One — this separates you… Act Two will take that to the next level… And Act Three is — I'd say — maybe the most powerful."
27. [Act 1 stakes + story] "Without this, generating sales and leads is just not going to happen."
28. [Signpost] "And that brings us on to Act 2 Storytelling."
29. [Signpost] "And then we move on to Act 3 Storytelling."
30. [Stakes/risk reframe] "But guess what? If that happens, you just have another painful story to share. So you can't lose."
31. [Close / next-video send + proof] "And if you want to start practicing storytelling, you can do it in a very simple video that keeps blowing up channels… Will take you 8 minutes to watch."

**What keeps it moving.** This is the longest script and Ed keeps it moving by nesting open loops. The intro promises "three different types of stories", but he withholds the three acts until roughly three quarters in, and repeatedly tells you they are coming ("before I break down the three formats that can make you millions… you need to understand…"). He demonstrates the technique on the viewer in real time (two versions of the same lesson, then "Can you feel the difference?") and then keeps calling back to the driveway story as the worked example for every foundation, so the proof and the teaching are the same object. Section boundaries are almost always marked with a superlative or a warning rather than a bare label ("This is probably my favorite thing", "that's critical you do not blow", "maybe the most powerful"). He pre-empts objections as conditional bridges ("if you're like a doctor… that's okay", "if you don't have clients… that's fine") so no viewer feels excluded from the next act. Every act and foundation is tied back to sales, clients or credibility within a sentence or two, and the close carries social proof ("they've already commented saying it blew up their channels").

## Video 5 · The EXACT System to make $10k From Youtube in 7 Days

1. [Hook / proof] "In 2023 I helped 40 people launch a simple product to their YouTube audience and the average one made $10,000 in its first seven days of its existence."
2. [Promise + desire] "So today I'm going to give you the proven system we used to make these channels an average of 10K in 7 days so you can start earning more."
3. [Rhetorical question / objection voiced] "First though—how did they make $10,000 in 7 days? That sounds like utter internet marketing talk."
4. [Proof / transparency + open loop] "Here's the raw transparency of it." … "But don't worry—I'm going to show you how to deal with that later"
5. [Contrarian / expectation setting] "So this isn't like a guaranteed magic internet money printer."
6. [Rhetorical question] "But how many viewers do you need to make $10,000 in the first seven days for launching?"
7. [Pain voiced + re-hook + proof] "Now you might be going 'Great, I'm miles off of that.' But check this out. So this is Air."
8. [Conditional bridge + proof] "So if you have a high ticket offer or service, you can do what Liam's been doing recently."
9. [Rhetorical question / grounding] "So what is this product? Well I call it a workshop or a masterass."
10. [Misconception + open loop] "this is not a course. I'm going to go into why courses are just a really bad idea later on."
11. [Objection + proof] "Which I know sounds like nobody's ever going to buy… But my first one years ago made $80,000 over its life."
12. [Signpost + stakes] "before we move on to the system to make these, there's one big mistake that is critical you avoid."
13. [Analogy / rhetorical question] "So if I asked you to build me a skyscraper, could you do it?" … "Can you build me a garden shed?"
14. [Signpost + stacked promises] "Okay so now let's get into the simple system… And then I'm going to show you what to do if your sales don't work out"
15. [Story lead-in] "All right so let's go back to my first ever workshop"
16. [Misconception] "Step two—everyone gets this wrong—because they go and make the product. Do not do that."
17. [Story + pattern interrupt] "And I thought, Damn, that's good. And the cool thing is—it worked."
18. [Warning / conditional] "Now a word of warning here:"
19. [Running re-hook] "Then we move on to the next step which is still not making it." / "the next step is—we still don't make it."
20. [Rhetorical question / re-hook] "Now do you want to know something crazy?"
21. [Signpost + desire] "So if you want to hit $10,000 in 7 days, you need to harness urgency."
22. [Misconception + story] "you have to avoid the trap you are almost guaranteed to fall into… So years ago, we were going to launch this Netflix-style product."
23. [Objection + pattern interrupt] "And I know what you're thinking—'Oh, you're going to annoy people.'… Apart from Steve. I miss you, Steve."
24. [Signpost] "Which brings us on to step nine. We just launch it and we wait and see."
25. [Rhetorical question / pain] "But what happens if you launch and this doesn't make you many sales—or any sales?"
26. [Rhetorical question / analogy] "But what if your viewers get annoyed with you for selling? Well—think of it like this."
27. [Close / next-video send + pattern interrupt] "watch this video next… so you can make the big cash monies. That was weird."

**What keeps it moving.** The spine of this script is a repeated denial. From step two onward Ed keeps telling the viewer "we still don't make it", which turns an ordinary list of steps into a running tease. The product itself becomes the payoff he keeps holding back until "you then are going to go on finally to producing your actual product." He front-loads doubt on purpose, voicing the viewer's scepticism ("That sounds like utter internet marketing talk", "Great, I'm miles off of that") and immediately answering it with small-channel proof (Air, under 900 subscribers), so the big claim feels earned rather than hyped. Early open loops are parked and paid off later, covering why courses are a bad idea, pricing, and "how to deal with that later" if the launch fails. Most sections open with a question the viewer might ask ("What do you charge?", "But what happens if you launch and this doesn't make you many sales?", "But what if your viewers get annoyed with you for selling?"). Two analogies (skyscraper vs garden shed, the Herbalife dinner guest) do both the grounding and the objection handling. Self-deprecating asides ("Apart from Steve. I miss you, Steve.", "That was weird.") break the pattern just as the teaching gets dense.

## Video 6 · Copy This Video, It’ll Blow Up Your Sales

1. [Hook / proof] "This video is one of the fastest and easiest I've ever made and one of the best performing for sales."
2. [Proof escalation] "it blew up my sales multiple times, used it in multiple videos, and got hundreds of emails too."
3. [Promise + desire] "So now I'm going to give you every step to make your own so that you can just copy it."
4. [Signpost / rhetorical question] "So to start — what is this video and why should you make it?"
5. [Contrarian] "anyone can copy a Google blog post and turn it into a video. But not everyone can point out a problem a viewer has"
6. [Grounding + desire] "you get your viewers to send in something for you to review… it will massively raise your credibility with undeniable proof of your genius."
7. [Proof] "look: made insane money."
8. [Rhetorical question / objection] "But can this style of video work for anyone?"
9. [Stakes] "So don’t just write it off… because it could be a massive opportunity for your business."
10. [Pain / story] "a while ago I wanted to post more often, but making videos takes up a huge amount of time, right?"
11. [Expectation reframe] "I don’t expect these videos to get crazy views or blow up… but what I want them to do really is convert my viewers to buyers."
12. [Transition] "All right — so now you know why you need to do these, here's the system to make your own."
13. [Step 1 grounding] "What you need to do is crack your title. But that can take ages. So I've just come up with three that you can use"
14. [Rhetorical question bridge] "But how do you get that number in the title nice and big…? Well this is where step two comes in"
15. [Step 3 open loop] "But there is a trick to making these images work well with your content"
16. [Step 4 promise] "stop this becoming a planning nightmare… give you the secret to sales success."
17. [Conditional reassurance] "But if you can’t visually show a transformation — don’t panic, because this can still work."
18. [Step 5 proof + promise] "I’m going to play the intro of the video that generated $71,000 — and then I’m going to break it down"
19. [Step 6 promise + proof] "the next step is going to get you a lot of emails. And you can see I’ve generated hundreds"
20. [Misconception / story proof] "But do not say… a thousand people entered it. But when I said 'Submit to my next roast' — it got 20 signups."
21. [Step 7 contrarian] "But you don’t have to write a script for the rest of the video if you don’t want to."
22. [Step 8 confession / curiosity] "which I think I’d do a bit differently now… Looking back, I think that was a mistake."
23. [Step 9 stakes + proof] "there’s another step that we can’t ignore. So look at this roast."
24. [Rhetorical question / stakes] "Otherwise, how on earth do you know what to repeat?"
25. [Close / next-video send] "then watch this playlist next"

**What keeps it moving.** This is a nine-step build, and Ed makes almost every step carry a payoff before it carries any instruction, as in "the next step’s going to stop this becoming a planning nightmare", "the next step is going to get you a lot of emails", "there’s another step that we can’t ignore." The steps are chained by questions rather than labels. "But how do you get that number in the title nice and big…?" is answered by "this is where step two comes in", which makes the next step feel like the answer to a problem the viewer has just been handed. Proof comes in hard, specific numbers ($71,000, 1,700 submissions, "a thousand people" vs "20 signups"), and he performs the method on himself by playing his own intro and taking it apart. Two confessions ("Looking back, I think that was a mistake", "YouTube was wrong") reopen tension late in the video, when most step lists go flat. The "don’t panic" conditionals ("even if you only have like 20 people", "if you can’t visually show a transformation") keep smaller or harder-to-fit channels watching.

## Video 7 · How I'm Using AI To Rapidly Grow My Channel And Business

1. [Hook / desire, imagine frame] "Imagine instantly knowing if your next video would flop before you made it and then being given a better idea"
2. [Pattern interrupt / reveal] "Well, this is the reality we now live in—and AI is doing that for me right now."
3. [Promise] "So in this video I'm going to show you some tools that are going to save your time a ton and help you generate more income from YouTube."
4. [Signpost + stakes] "So let's start with our first AI, and that's going to stop you wasting time and making a video that won't generate you views or sales."
5. [Analogy / pain] "it's guardrails on a bowling alley… this kind of spray-and-pray approach means the ball often ends up in the gully"
6. [Desire] "Meaning every video will grow your business, not just waste your time."
7. [Demo set-up + pattern interrupt] "feed an idea into it I've come up with for my own channel—now that I kind of know sucks"
8. [Empathy / re-hook] "So check this out. When you are raging about how unfair YouTube is, I'm going to put the flop in"
9. [Open loop] "Now at the end of the video I'm going to show you what this AI is."
10. [Signpost + proof] "The next use of this AI, which has saved me—I don't know, like half an hour to an hour every video I make"
11. [Result] "So you can progress years pushing a button."
12. [Signpost + curiosity] "This next AI used to do something in 30 minutes that usually takes me a day or two."
13. [Story + pain] "I needed to hire a new team member, and it was one of those really important tasks that I just didn't want to do"
14. [Re-hook / complication] "but then I realized I’d shot myself in the foot here."
15. [Payoff / pattern interrupt] "and guess who I hired? Rouser. It was absolutely spot on."
16. [Reframe / grounding] "So this isn't just about hiring people. This AI is about asking yourself: what am I about to do that I don't want to?"
17. [Signpost + proof] "the next way to use AI, which generated me thousands of emails in a few minutes' work."
18. [Signpost + desire] "Which brings me on to another way to use AI to earn more and work less."
19. [Pain / story] "So I have a problem. I can't just pump loads of people into my program"
20. [Proof] "the number one issue that everyone used to struggle with is now not even in our top five asked questions."
21. [Objection pre-empt] "Now before you think "Oh Ed's gone all AI""
22. [Loop close] "And that brings us on to the AI I showed you at the very start. What is it? Where did it come from?"
23. [Stakes] "I honestly think this is the difference between making millions and going to zero over the next 2 years."
24. [Story sequence] school computer, card payments, vlogging, Bitcoin
25. [Stakes] "you're just putting yourself in the "I'm going to get in too late and then regret it later" club."
26. [Rhetorical question + action] "So where do you start if you don't want to join the billions of people…?"
27. [Close / next-video send] "then watch this video next… Please don't sleep on it. You've been warned."

**What keeps it moving.** This script is held together by one long open loop. The "imagine" demo in the intro is withheld with "at the end of the video I'm going to show you what this AI is" and only paid off after four more use cases ("And that brings us on to the AI I showed you at the very start. What is it?"). Each AI section opens on a time-saved or result number ("half an hour to an hour every video", "30 minutes that usually takes me a day or two", "thousands of emails in a few minutes' work"), so the signpost carries the proof. The middle runs on small stories with a twist. The hiring story sets up a complication ("I’d shot myself in the foot"), a moral dilemma, and then a punchline ("guess who I hired? Rouser."). Ed heads off the obvious objection ("before you think 'Oh Ed's gone all AI'") before he raises the stakes. The close is the highest-stakes part of the video, a string of historical stories (school computer, card payments, vlogging, Bitcoin) leading to "making millions and going to zero", with fear of missing out as the send ("You've been warned.").

## Video 8 · EXACTLY How To Bring Viewers Back To Your Channel

1. [Hook / rhetorical question] "Why do people get addicted to things?"
2. [Grounding + desire] "that feeling is what you want to create with your YouTube videos if you want to build a red-hot community of return viewers who then go on to buy your thing."
3. [Promise + proof] "my five-step process… to bring millions of people back to my content over and over again."
4. [Proof / pattern interrupt] "And it didn't work once. It worked here. It worked here. It worked here."
5. [Signpost + curiosity gap] "before we get on to the steps, why is what I'm about to show you so incredibly powerful?"
6. [Analogy / empathy] "have you ever really craved chocolate… Just a sad little chocolate rice cake"
7. [Stakes chain] "they don't watch your video… And they don't come back. And if that happens, you won't make any sales."
8. [Desire] "you just build yourself a drawer full of never ending chocolate viewers binge on. And that is the secret to sales and views."
9. [Grounding / demo] "I'm going to walk you through a demo channel in the recruitment space."
10. [Step 1 stakes] "Step One of this system is absolutely critical—because if you get this wrong, nothing else will work."
11. [Misconception] "most of the YouTube education out there is telling you to make what works for those youngsters"
12. [Proof] "This is Azul. He exploded to 100,000 subscribers really quick"
13. [Step 2 + callback] "Viewers clicked on a Mars bar—and they got given a rice cake."
14. [Empathy / rhetorical question] "And you've wondered—how on earth are they getting so many views?"
15. [Rhetorical question / comment prompt] "neon hoodie… Or from someone who's sitting behind a mahogany desk? Let me know which one you would trust more in the comments."
16. [Step 3 signpost + callback] "keep them coming back to the chocolate roll."
17. [Proof contrast + rhetorical question] "How is Dan Co… competing with someone like, well, Mosy…?"
18. [Misconception] "save you from one of the biggest YouTube mistakes out there: Chasing a flat perfect retention line."
19. [Story lead-in] "But let's go back a couple of years."
20. [Pattern interrupt] "I literally pay Greg—who edits for me—not to edit for me as much. Sorry Greg."
21. [Rhetorical questions / reframe] "Did I get more comments? Did I get more shares? Did I get more email signups or product sales?"
22. [Stakes] "that's the difference between struggling and scaling."
23. [Signpost + stakes] "Which brings us onto our next step… this part is a non-negotiable."
24. [Story / proof] "So when I pivoted from my old channel, Film Booth"
25. [Pattern interrupt] "Choo choo. Oh no, that’s bad. Still going to workshop that slogan."
26. [Re-hook / warning] "But here’s the thing: If you find one thing that works, you can’t just repeat it over and over again forever."
27. [Misconception] "Now here’s the part most people get wrong"
28. [Summary + close] "So now you understand why viewer expectations matter so much… Watch this video next"
29. [Pattern interrupt / callback] "Chocolate talk’s getting me hungry. Flipping rice cake."

**What keeps it moving.** One extended metaphor (the chocolate drawer, the Mars bar vs the rice cake) runs through this whole script. Ed sets it up before the steps, then reuses it as a callback in almost every section ("Viewers clicked on a Mars bar—and they got given a rice cake", "the chocolate roll", "that chocolate drawer effect", "eating the same chocolate"), and ends on it as a joke ("Flipping rice cake."). Because it is always the same image, each new step feels like part of one idea rather than a new list item. A single demo channel (the 45-to-55-year-old retirement/recruitment viewer) also returns at the end of every step ("So for our retirement channel…"), which turns abstract advice into a concrete application each time. Rhetorical questions do a lot of the work, with stacked A/B/C options ("neon hoodie… or mahogany desk?", "calm and collected? … dancing?") and prompts to comment make the viewer take part. The last two sections are re-hooked with warnings ("But here’s the thing:", "Now here’s the part most people get wrong:") that complicate the step just when it seemed finished. Self-mocking asides (paying Greg not to edit, "Choo choo. Oh no, that’s bad.") break the pattern between denser teaching.

## Video 9 · Copy This YouTube Strategy, It'll Blow Up Your Business

1. [Hook / pain + stakes] "Most businesses waste their time and miss out on a ton of consistent sales from YouTube because they overlook the six critical steps…"
2. [Promise + proof] "I'm going to show you the exact sales system I use to drive thousands of leads, calls and make $1.2 million in the first 3 months of 2025 alone without any ads."
3. [Pattern interrupt / bonus promise] "And oh yeah — it's going to get you more views too."
4. [Signpost / grounding] "to start, you need to understand the strategy we're going to use to generate sales and why it is so powerful. So let me show you behind the scenes of my channel"
5. [Grounding / proof] "check out this views per hour metric down the side here… three or four videos are pulling in 70 to 100 views an hour all the time"
6. [Empathy / conditional] "to some of you it's going to feel like a lot. To others, not that many."
7. [Grounding] "these four videos are basically referring each other all of the time… because they work as a team like this."
8. [Re-hook + desire] "But that's not the best thing about this for businesses — because imagine if each of these videos was optimized to generate sales and leads as well."
9. [Promise] "That's exactly what I'm going to show you how to build right now with a very simple six-step strategy"
10. [Signpost + stakes] "step one — this is really important because if this fails, you can't really start the system in the first place."
11. [Demonstration / callback] "here's my script and I'm going to read it out and then I'm going to pull out what those three things are"
12. [Signpost + result] "step two — which is going to rehook viewers and it's going to get them past the first third of a video."
13. [Desire] "the end is where the sales and leads magic happens. So we really need to get them there."
14. [Signpost + grounding] "step three — which is the bridge from views to leads and sales."
15. [Rhetorical question / curiosity gap] "The second one has less views overall — yet it brings in way more clicks and leads. Why is this happening?"
16. [Signpost + pain] "step four of this system — which is the solution to a massive problem all of us face."
17. [Rhetorical question / contrarian] "But how can you possibly know what viewers already know to get around this? Well — you can't."
18. [Story / proof] "in this example, instead of just repeating the obvious… what I did was create a simple spreadsheet"
19. [Objection pre-empt] "But do you have to build a spreadsheet like I did? Well, no."
20. [Signpost + curiosity] "Now the next step is deceptively simple — but incredibly powerful."
21. [Rhetorical question / proof] "this tiny little tweak is the main reason why this video here got way more leads than this other one. What did I do differently?"
22. [Re-hook / bonus] "And if you really want to turbocharge signups in your videos — here's what else tracking my videos has taught me."
23. [Signpost + proof] "it's the glue that holds the referral network together… and look what it's done for my own. It's crazy, right?"
24. [Result] "you create this automated sales system that blows up your email list, calls, leads, and sales."
25. [Summary / promise] "If you include these six steps in every video you make, I can pretty much guarantee you're going to grow your business."
26. [Close / next-video send] "So watch this video next and I'll show you exactly how to turn viewers into buyers step by step."

**What keeps it moving.** Ed turns the whole video into its own demonstration. In Step 1 he re-reads the script's opening and points at "this first line" and "the second line", so the viewer realises the intro they just sat through was built on the method. The pre-steps section establishes one concrete image (four videos "referring each other" at 70-100 views an hour) and every later step is tied back to it ("imagine if every video in your referral network did this", "which wrecks the referral network system"), which gives six separate tactics a single through-line. Each step label is loaded with a benefit or stakes clause rather than left bare ("which is going to rehook viewers", "the bridge from views to leads and sales", "the solution to a massive problem all of us face", "the glue that holds the referral network together"). Proof arrives as side-by-side comparisons with a question attached ("Why is this happening?", "What did I do differently?"), so each data point opens a small loop that the step then closes. Objections are raised and dismissed in one beat ("Well — you can't. But that doesn't matter", "Well, no."), keeping momentum without detours.

## Video 10 · How To Make A Killer Video (That Blows Up Your Sales)

1. [Hook / proof] "This video made over $240,000 in 90 days."
2. [Curiosity gap] "And one surprising lesson I learned making it proves that this could be the easiest and most efficient video for generating sales on YouTube."
3. [Promise + desire] "I'm going to show you the exact system I use… so that you can make your own high income video that isn't as reliant on the algorithm as you might think"
4. [Rhetorical question / grounding] "what is this video and why could it potentially save you hours and get you amazing results, too?"
5. [Contrarian naming] "Well, I call it antiarketing. Why? Well, because what I'm about to show you kind of goes against some of the most common marketing advice"
6. [Misconception setup] "Now, we're told this is powerful because this gated video is supposed to warm viewers up"
7. [Proof + stakes] "I own a software company called Red Track… the average click rate on a lead magnet link below a video is 1 to 3%."
8. [Stakes escalation] "But it gets even worse… we have 150 left. And then it gets even worse… Meaning we can now warm up 45 people from the initial 10,000."
9. [Story pivot] "that kind of feels a little bit broken to me. So, I thought, wait a second."
10. [Loop close + promise] "And that's how this anti-marketing video made me $240,000 in 90 days… I'm also going to address if it means nobody should even build a system like this anymore"
11. [Signpost + analogy] "step one… If you want to go on holiday, what's the first thing you plan?"
12. [Open loop] "And as you're going to see later, we don't actually need YouTube to promote our video"
13. [Signpost + grounding] "step two… there are three formats that you could pick from."
14. [Proof + twist] "it made me $140,000 in 2 days… But I decided not to use it again"
15. [Re-hook] "But this is where it gets interesting because when I tried it…"
16. [Analogy / grounding] "you know how software companies they'll give you like a 7-day free trial… I've named this the premium video system."
17. [Conditional bridge] "Now, if you don't have a program or service, you can't do this. So this is the third option"
18. [Open loop] "I'll go into more detail on how to plan these out in a minute."
19. [Signpost + stakes] "the next step, which is no doubt the highest stakes part of this entire process… if we mess this up… we can't get any views on this thing at all."
20. [Signpost / pain] "step five… I find it really hard to plan a video"
21. [Re-hook + proof] "the next step is very important because it means this video didn't make me $240,000. It was actually more like half a million"
22. [Rhetorical question / story] "But do you write a script or do you present it from bullet points?"
23. [Signpost + desire] "two critical steps that will massively increase the amount of dollars per view"
24. [Stakes] "you're just going to wreck your results. You're going to get no clicks. You're going to lose out on sales."
25. [Loop close + result] "the algorithm is no longer fully in control of this video… we kind of have a magic internet money printer here."
26. [Close / next-video send] "if you want to study my $240,000 video to see exactly what I included in it. Click the video right here"

**What keeps it moving.** The whole script hangs off one number in the first sentence ($240,000 in 90 days), which Ed returns to as a refrain ("my almost quart of a million dollar video", "This is my $240,000 video") and then escalates mid-script ("It was actually more like half a million"), so the proof itself works as a re-hook. The setup before Step 1 is a funnel-leak stakes ladder ("But it gets even worse… And then it gets even worse") that makes the conventional method feel broken before he offers the alternative. He names his contrarian idea early ("antiarketing") and asks "Why?" of it, then opens two loops he deliberately closes much later, "as you're going to see later, we don't actually need YouTube to promote our video" (paid off by the end-screen step) and "if it means nobody should even build a system like this anymore" (paid off with "we do still want to build this kind of old way system"). Step labels carry stakes words ("the highest stakes part of this entire process", "two critical steps", "very important because…") and every option is judged against sales and clients rather than views. The failure story about filming for 6 hours and editing for a day is used as candid empathy that makes the advice ("just don't rush it") feel earned.

## Video 11 · EVERYTHING You Need To Make Sales On YouTube

1. [Hook / rhetorical question] "Have you ever wondered if your YouTube channel's actually ready to make money?"
2. [Proof / story lead-in] "over the last 18 months I've been helping people launch businesses from their channels and many have flown, but some didn't do as well."
3. [Promise] "So in this video I'm going to show you exactly what they are and how to check them for yourself."
4. [Signpost / open loop] "But before we get on to that, you first need to work out if your niche can actually make you money in the first place."
5. [Grounding] "I want you to check out this triangle… the width of this triangle represents your audience size"
6. [Desire] "And this is how people make money from YouTube with less views."
7. [Rhetorical question / signpost] "So what you need to do is ask yourself: which one do you fall into?"
8. [Story / example + rhetorical questions] "Why the World's Best Golfers Always Wear Red… I wonder why someone would wear red. What would that do? Should I wear red?"
9. [Signpost] "So let's move on to the next tier up."
10. [Misconception + proof] "traditionally people said these niches were not worth that much money… until I started working with more people in it and witnessed them charging from $6,000 to $30,000"
11. [Pattern interrupt] "but just shut up, golf nerd. Stop bashing me in the comments, it doesn't matter. Let me make my point."
12. [Empathy / reassurance] "Now don't panic if you're not in the top… Don't go telling yourself you can't make this work because you teach people knitting. Trust me. You can."
13. [Summary + signpost] "Okay, so now you know how valuable your niche might be, let's have a look at how to spot some signs that you have a very hot audience"
14. [Signpost + curiosity] "the first sign—it's kind of weird—but to me it's one of the most clear-cut signs"
15. [Story] "10 seconds later I'd get four comments saying "First!""
16. [Signpost / story] "The second sign is this… how can you like it? You've not finished it yet!"
17. [Curiosity gap] "The next is what I personally think is the most important metric on YouTube… Nobody ever really talks about it."
18. [Proof / grounding] "the book Contagious… And they called this social currency."
19. [Re-hook / warning] "But before you go, "Oh goody!", you need to listen to the rest of these things, 'cause it's not quite as simple as that."
20. [Analogy / grounding] "think of your audience size as a bucket with a hole in it"
21. [Signpost + story + stakes] "next we have the boring sign absolutely nobody wants to hear… a couple of my clients' launches didn't work out as planned."
22. [Rhetorical question] "who do you trust? The person you've seen on YouTube once, or the one that you've been tuning into for years?"
23. [Signposts through the remaining signs] "Which brings me on to the next metric" / "The next sign is a continuation of this" / "Now the next is a metric we can track off of YouTube"
24. [Contrarian] "believe it or not—your hottest fans are not on YouTube."
25. [Result] "Now the next sign is like someone nailed a flipping neon one to your head… That really is the ultimate sign."
26. [Rhetorical question / conditional] "But what if you're not seeing these signs—or many of them?"
27. [Close / next-video send] "if you want to learn how to make videos that do everything I've just said, watch this video next"

**What keeps it moving.** This is a list video (a sequence of "signs"), and Ed stops a list from feeling flat by giving almost every item an adjective or ranking before he explains it, like "kind of weird", "the most important metric on YouTube", "really simple", "the boring sign absolutely nobody wants to hear", "like someone nailed a flipping neon one to your head", "the ultimate sign". He front-loads a gate ("But before we get on to that, you first need to work out if your niche can actually make you money") so the viewer has to place themselves on the triangle before the promised signs arrive, and then reassures anyone who might drop off ("Now don't panic if you're not in the top… Trust me. You can."). The mid-video warning "But before you go, "Oh goody!"… it's not quite as simple as that" is a deliberate re-hook at the point where a viewer might think they have their answer. Signs are carried by short first-person stories (the "First!" comments, likes before the video could be finished, his clients' failed launches and his own smugness), so each metric arrives with an emotion attached. The close flips to the viewer who is not seeing the signs, using a stack of "if you're…" conditionals to name their likely pain before the next-video send.

## Video 12 · Why Businesses Must Start A YouTube Channel (Right Now)

1. [Hook / rhetorical question + pain] "Have you ever dreamt of growing a YouTube channel in a business, but there's just something stopping you from doing it?"
2. [Story lead-in / proof] "So, I polled my community, and I was horrified when most people said their biggest concern about YouTube was not earning enough to pay back their time investment."
3. [Contrarian] "This is the worst reason for not starting a channel."
4. [Promise + desire] "not only will you get back everything you put in, you will get a hundred times more. And then I'm going to give you three more reasons"
5. [Rhetorical question / signpost] "All right, so why is the fear such an irrational fear that makes no sense? Well, it's because of this."
6. [Story / pain] "If we go back to 2019, I had a video production company and every month we started from zero"
7. [Pain escalation] "And then worse, when I did get paid… this isn't enough."
8. [Story turn / desire] "These guys are amazing… I bet they never have to chase people for sales."
9. [Desire / north star] "if I can get 500,000 subscribers, I might be able to generate a quarter of a million pounds… I won't feel so trapped."
10. [Signpost / lesson + reframe] "And this brings us on to our first lesson here… The real fear I had was, if I don't do this, what will change?"
11. [Pain / empathy] "they go back to their life that they're not happy with, and they go back to chasing clients"
12. [Stakes] "the real risk of not starting a YouTube channel is being in the same place in 10 years' time."
13. [Story / next problem] "I had another problem: I've got to run the business, I've got to make money, and I've got to try and grow a channel."
14. [Reframe] "if this really hurts for three years, or even ten, at least I've got thirty or forty years left"
15. [Story low point] "I was posting three videos a week and I was getting absolutely nowhere… "This is dumb. It doesn't work.""
16. [Turn] "So, I changed strategy… and it blew up the channel… And that is when everything changed."
17. [Misconception / lesson] "Now, this is what people don't understand about business or YouTube. We always assume the worst."
18. [Rhetorical question / analogy] "even if you banged your head against a brick wall for ten years, eventually your head's going to go through it, right?"
19. [Proof escalation] "the biggest channels in the niche are emailing me. Then I've started to get invited onto podcasts."
20. [Proof / result] "it doubled every year until we capped out at like 4 million this year… I'm about fifty years ahead of where I ever thought I would be."
21. [Desire stack] "You will massively underestimate what is possible here. You'll get tons of sponsorship requests…"
22. [Signpost + stakes / urgency] "And then the final reason you kind of need to start today is: today is the easiest YouTube will ever be—ever again."
23. [Stakes / challenge] "And if you're not very happy, and if you don't see much future potential in your life, that's on you."
24. [Close / next-video send] "And if you need any more inspiration, just watch this video here."

**What keeps it moving.** Unlike the step-based scripts, this one is a single chronological story (2019 → north star → 5 a.m. starts → 18 months of nothing → Film Booth → full-time → 4 million), and the movement comes from the story's own low and high points rather than from step labels. Ed opens with a viewer-directed question and a poll result, labels the audience's fear as "the worst reason", and then uses the story to reframe the stakes from "will it pay me back?" to "if I don't do this, what will change?", which is repeated almost verbatim later ("what's going to happen to me?") as a callback. The pain section is unusually long and concrete (the £22,000 monthly target, "Dave's too busy", "once the tax man's taken his bit"), so the later results land as relief. The promised "three more reasons" are only loosely signposted ("our first lesson here", "the final reason"), with the misconception line "this is what people don't understand about business or YouTube" carrying the middle. The close hardens into urgency and personal responsibility ("today is the easiest YouTube will ever be", "that's on you") before a softer inspiration-based send to the next video.

# Part 4 · The scripts in full

Verbatim from the Notion page, including Ed's punctuation and typos.

## Video 1 · I Turned OFF My YouTube Ads, Maybe You Should Too

Something weird has happened that's completely changed the way I think about YouTube.
And that is — the other day I was looking at my data, and I did some maths… and it occurred to me that YouTube ads that play before my videos could actually be impacting my sales and my business.
So I turned them off immediately.
So in this video, I'm going to show you why I think leaving YouTube ads on your videos could be costing you money — and I'm going to help you work out if you should turn them off too.
All right, so why did I turn YouTube ads off?
Well, let's just start with the basics before I give you a bit of a shock as to just how bad leaving ads on your videos could be.
All right — so imagine if every Coke can had a Pepsi logo on the side. That would be absolutely insane, right? Because Coke would just never advertise their competition.
Thing is — this is kind of how YouTube ads work.
Your competition can potentially appear at the start of your video, trying to get viewers to watch their content, click on their offer, and buy their thing.
So that's the basics of the problem… which is kind of only scratching the surface of the damage it might be doing.
So let me show you the bad news.
I'm going to use my own business and channel as a demo to show you what's potentially going on here.
All right — so in the first 5 months of 2025, I generated over $2 million from the traffic YouTube drives to my offer, my sales system.
And at the same time, I made $6,000 from ads. Which is a nice bit of pocket money, right?
The thing is — I track all of my sales metrics from YouTube.
And this metric here in particular, I really like — it's called dollar per view.
Now, dollar per view divides the amount of views that a video has against the amount of income it's generated to reveal kind of its true sales power.
So you can see this one here, it's making like $1 per view.
And this one's making around $2.
And this one's making over $3 per view.
But let's just take this video here that makes me over $3 every view to continue the demo.
Now, when I started thinking about how ads could actually be harming my business — because of what I told you — I decided to look at what percentage of my viewers they could be driving away from my content.
I did some research, and a general ballpark is that 0.5 to 1.5% of viewers who see an ad on YouTube will click it.
So based on that…
This video here got 14,679 views.
And if 0.5% of the viewers clicked the ad before this video and went away —
based on the fact that every view this video gets has made me over $3 —
I've lost $260 in sales.
But if that was 1.5% — the upper end of the average —
I'm down $790.
The thing is — that's just one video.
So if we look at my channel as a whole since the start of 2025 (which is about 5 months at the time of filming),
it's gotten 1.44 million views in that period.
And my channel average dollar per view is about $1.33.
So if 0.5% of those viewers decided to click an ad —
that's 7,000 views taken away from my channel.
At the cost of $9,310 to my business.
Or if 1.5% clicked —
I've lost out on $27,930.
What that means is — if we do a little profit and loss with the amount of ad revenue I made…
The overall opportunity cost to my business could be up to $21,000
in just the first 5 months of the year.
Now of course — these sums are just an example. They're by no way an exact science.
But we also could factor in that some people will click play and then see an ad — and potentially bail — because they don't want to watch an advert that much.
So to me, in my business — it doesn't really make sense to have them on.
But I guess the question is… should you turn them off too?
Well — no.
Maybe not.
And I'll explain.
So about 4 to 5 years ago, I had a channel that got way more views than this one you're watching.
And it used to make me about $4,000 to $5,000 a month from ads.
And at that time, that was kind of replacing my wage.
I could live off of that — and it was a considerable amount. It still is.
I was also selling other simple products too — but if I turned the ads off?
Well, that would have probably stopped me being able to run the channel.
And it would have wrecked the leg-up that income gave me —
because it was the thing that enabled me to then spend the time building products and services that would eventually generate millions.
So if you're in a position where your ad revenue makes a considerable difference to your life —
no, do not turn it off.
But if you're in a position where you have a business like mine that's generating sales —
