all you do is you take the data that you have that shows you how much YouTube is generating you…
And then you divide it by your views that month.
You then have a rough dollar per view average for your channel.
Then you times your monthly views by 0.5%,
which is — remember — the lower estimate for the amount of people that will click on the ads before it.
And then when you get that number —
you times it by your average dollar per view.
And it's going to reveal the potential cost of ads to your business.
You can then make the decision from there.
And if you want to get your dollar per view up —
so you can earn way more money from less views —
then watch this video next, and I'm going to show you exactly how to make your channel more profitable in the next 90 days.

## Video 2 · Copy This YouTube Studio Style, It'll Blow Up Your Business

The second your video loads, people judge you. And if they don't like what they see, they're going to leave. And you're never going to generate views and sales.
But that doesn't mean you need to spend a ton of cash on production kit to make an amazing first impression. And I know this because I've made millions from my videos using just this battered old tiny light.
So in this video, I'm going to give you my really simple production system that I've been using for years to film videos that are going to instantly level up the first impression you give your viewers — so the ideal clients watch you for longer and turn to buyers faster.
Okay, so to start, we just need to look at the common misconception about video quality. And that is: one person's going to tell you it really matters and that you should make videos that kind of look like mine, right? And then one is going to say it doesn't matter and you should make videos look like this.
The truth is, we're all completely wrong — but also right at the same time. Because there is just no broad one-size-fits-all YouTube advice for your production quality that's going to work for everyone. Viewers want different things.
Instead, what you have to do is figure out what level of production works for your viewers — based on your weak spots. And to do that, you're going to use this chart.
So what you can see here are four pillars. And to get results on YouTube, all you need to do is get three of four of them above what I call the high-quality line. And when you do that? You won't turn viewers off.
So: Pillar one — this is your presenting ability. Basically, how well you come across on camera. Pillar two is your communication ability. This is how easy you make things to understand. Pillar three is your production quality — just the visuals. And pillar four is your credibility. This is basically coming across as an expert fast.
So this is Chris Do. He's a multimillionaire. He's got loads of followers. And he has taught for so long — he is just a world-class expert on his topic. But not only that — he's an expert in communicating. And he can present at an amazing level.
What that means is: Chris's chart looks like this. See these three pillars are super high. And what that means is — he could cut back a ton on the fourth, production quality, if he wanted. And just make a video of nothing other than him riffing for 20 minutes over a Google Doc. And that could still grow his following and business. Because of his sheer brilliance.
But this is Steve's chart. Steve unfortunately decided to follow the advice of presenting a scrappy-looking video from a Google Doc. And the thing is — his chart looks like this. He is credible, sure. But he's never presented before, so this pillar is just rock bottom. And he's never really worked out how to simplify his information so others can understand it that well. So his communication pillar is not great too.
What that means is — if he keeps his production quality low, he just stands no chance.
The thing is: when it comes to YouTube, most people start with a chart that looks like this. And getting presentation and communication and credibility above that line? It could take ages. 50 videos, maybe.
So the fastest one to fix and level up — is production quality.
So looking at this, you can work out: well, do I need to make any changes? And if you think you need to raise a pillar fast, continue to watch the video — because now I'm going to give you the world's simplest system to level up this column fast.
All right. So Step 1 is all about this.
So what you can see on screen right now is: I'm in the same environment — yet the vibe and quality looks so different, right? And the only thing that has changed is the light.
No light makes me look pretty crap and boring. Putting the light straight in front of me kind of removes that moody feeling. Switching the light behind me to white instead of warm, and then opening my curtains gives this video a totally different vibe too. I quite like that. Looks all right.
So you first want to invest in just a light. But it doesn't have to be like a $2,000 beast.
For 18 months, I lived in Airbnbs and I was traveling the world. And the only light I could fit in my bag was this thing. Meaning all of these videos that you can see on screen right now are produced using the same system I'm going to show you — but with this one battered old light.
Would I buy that? Well — no. I only had that because I was a homeless man who couldn't fit anything else in my bag.
But now I'm not a bum — I use this thing. It's got this big soft box on it — and what that means is, it makes my image look much softer and a lot more pro. So if you have the space, I would highly recommend getting what's called a COB light like this with a soft box. You put it on it — and then I just put it here — so the light is hitting my face.
And what that's doing is it's putting some shadow on the opposite side — which kind of gives me this nice cinematic look. That's it right there. And if you don't like that — well you can just put the light behind the camera, pointed flat at your face instead. And that's all I have done in these examples you can see on screen. Just had one light there.
Which brings us on to Step 2.
So which of these videos on screen right now was shot on a $5,000 camera — and which one was shot on a 10-year-old camera that once had an entire pint of beer spilled on it… so all the buttons are stuck down and broken?
Well the pros can probably tell the difference — but I kind of struggle if I'm honest.
And that's because the light is the thing that levels up a camera's quality so much — and can still make a 10-year-old, beer-stained camera look amazing.
So: don't go and buy a new camera. Only upgrade after you've got a new light.
And don't do what I did and spend $5,000 on a camera — because it is complete overkill when an old Sony A6500 can do a very similar job.
Now a lot of my clients use this thing on screen — I believe it's called the Sony EV10. If I've got that wrong, I'll change it in text. And if I'm honest — it looks so good I'm not sure anyone else needs much more to get this system to work.
Really, all that matters is you have a camera that can create like a bit of a blurred background. And you're going to find out why in a minute.
Which brings us on to Step 3.
So have you ever been invited to someone's house — and walked in and gone, "Oh my gosh… this person lives in an absolute pigsty. What a tip." And you kind of want to leave, right? Because you're worried there's a rat infestation?
Well, your videos have the same effect — if your background is a complete mess.
So when it comes to your backdrop, all we really need to do is something that firstly doesn't give that messy, disgusting home feeling — but also secondly, doesn't distract people so they listen to you.
We don't want them going, "Oh they've got some pink wafer biscuits in the background, I like them," because they'll stop listening to your video.
But this doesn't mean you have to go and build a really fancy studio straight away — especially if you're not sure you're even going to be doing YouTube for the long term.
Instead, what I think most people should do is what I did when I was homeless and lived in Airbnbs — because I had to come up with new sets every month as I changed location.
Now at the start, I was getting really frustrated having to spend hours trying to figure out what the best look was. But after a few months, I realized I could always make something high quality so long as I stuck to a few time-saving rules of creating a set.
Rule number one was to not try and do anything fancy — and just to go: is there anything that would just work as a backdrop straight away? Like, what's kind of interesting?
This Airbnb was the only option available for hire and it was horrific. And I thought: there's no way I can film in here, it looks awful. But it had this like big black image of buildings on the wall — so I shot against that. And it looks pretty good, right?
And then in this place, the kitchen had this cool black textured effect. So I shot the breakfast bar in front of it. That's actually a fridge behind me, and there's a sink to my right there too.
This place is just a hotel room in LA and I just shot at the foot of my bed — I'm literally slammed up against the bed there. I just had the sofa and the TV behind me.
And right now — believe it or not — one sec let me show you...
This is my kitchen sink. I can literally reach all my dirty washing from that. Illusion ruined.
But it's not just me with this kind of like, "I'll just film in my house" setup thing. If you look, you can see Nish has generated millions of views filming in what looks like a home office. Deya seems to be filming in a living room. And this is why I think a camera that allows you to create this kind of blur effect behind you is important — because it will hide all the imperfections, and it can help make anything look more slick when you combine it with one good light.
But sometimes there wasn't a backdrop that was acceptable in these Airbnbs — so I came up with rule number two, which required a little bit more work, but not that much. And that was: I would just go to a plain wall. I'd set up my camera and my light in the exact same way I've shown you. And then I'd just slide some stuff into the background.
So here the Airbnb had this dark wall, so I slid in a plant to one side and then a light that was lying around to the other — to keep it nice and symmetrical. And that's it.
And if you look at Mark Tilbury — pretty sure he just has like a fake wooden wall there and a plant to one side and a jukebox to the other. It's really minimal. And he gets millions of views.
So not having a cool set really shouldn't stop you making content — because you probably have one that's good enough just ready and waiting.
Which brings us on to Step 4.
So one thing that can really wreck a video is if it sounds awful. Now traditionally, people spend forever soundproofing their sets and their studios so they can remove echo and have this crisp noise coming out of their mouth.
But when you're a homeless man living in Airbnbs — you can't do that. Because Airbnbs are often about minimal soft furnishings — so every room is just a nightmare for echo.
So the first thing I did to get around this was I just used a mic like this. These mics pick up way less echo, whereas the mics you clip on to your t-shirt pick up a ton.
The thing is — when I was homeless, moving around — I broke this mic. So it hisses all of the time. But instead of getting a new one — because I'm so flipping cheap — all I do is put the audio into Adobe's AI audio fixer. And it removes the echo and the hiss and makes it way better. So I just use a broken mic.
So before you go nailing a ton of sponges to your wall and worry about echo and sound — just don't buy anything. And try out the AI fixer.
Which brings us on to Step 5 — which is going to stop you hitting record and watching the footage back and going: "Fucking god, no." — when you realize it's all out of focus and that the battery died halfway through.
Now on many cameras you have a little screen you can flip around just to check you're in focus and the battery hasn't died. But it's pretty far away from me, and when I'm presenting, I'm in the zone — so I don't notice the battery dies and suddenly the screen turns off.
So all I do is I plug my camera into my laptop using one of these. This is called a Cam Link. And then I turn on Zoom — the free video calling platform. I start a meeting — and then I basically have myself on screen. And my laptop becomes a giant monitor — which is one reason why I always have it in shot now.
But you don't have to have it in shot — you could leave the laptop out of shot too, and you've still got it there. It's much easier.
Now this next step is optional — but hang on, let me show you the difference it makes.
All right — just turn that light off. You see how I suddenly look a little bit less like 3D and slick? And that's because — wait, I'll turn it back on — so that light there just pointing at like the back of my head?
This is known as a hair light. It kind of separates you from the background. But what makes it level up your content a little bit more is — look, I have these lights right behind me. And what this hair light does is: it makes it look like the lights that are on my wall are actually casting onto the back of my head.
So it adds depth without feeling really fake. So if you do have space, and you can be bothered, you can put a hair light on.
The thing is — once you've shot this, you then need to edit it to make sure you don't do anything that puts buyers off there.
So watch this video next, where I'm going to show you the simplest editing system in the world — even a beginner could do — and it's helped me make millions.

## Video 3 · How To Build A Profitable YouTube Channel In 30 Days

Most businesses struggle to generate consistent sales and leads from YouTube because they are stuck using outdated, complex, and time-consuming methods that don't really work. So today, I'm going to show you the six-step process responsible for millions in sales for myself, my clients, and how to set it up to flip even a small channel's profitability in just 30 days.
Step 1
You need to understand the viewer cycle and how to use it to make your channel more profitable. And to do that, what you need to understand is this chart here, which is going to change the way you think about growing a business on YouTube.
So on the left here, we have the number of interested viewers. Going at the top, this is like the number of people who are actively trying to solve a problem or get a result—whether it is learning a language, mastering a guitar, or growing a YouTube channel. On the bottom here, we've got the time after they discovered you—so months one, two, and three.
Now, what you want to do is think about what happens when you find a new creator who you really like. You binge five or six of her videos, and then next week, YouTube shows you their latest one, and you click it right away. And that keeps happening—until one day you realize, "You know what? I think I've got what I needed from this channel." So you just stop watching and you move on, because either you've solved the problem or you've kind of given up.
This is the viewer cycle, and it's happening on your channel right now. And what that means is: to make your channel as profitable as possible, we have this 30-day window here where viewers are probably their most interested in solving a problem—and there's a lot of them. And then after that, your potential buyer pool starts to drop off pretty fast.
So if you want to build a profitable channel—not just a popular one—you need to make sure everyone in this zone gets put through a system that converts them to buyers.
So Step 1 is to simply accept the truth: that even if you're amazing, most viewers will flake—not because of you, but because life moves on. And then what you want to do is find out how long, on average, your actual buyers watch you for before they bought.
Because if most of them buy in month three, that tells you you're missing out on all of these people and you need to fix your sales system. But how do you find out how long people have been watching?
Well, it's actually really easy. Just add this question to your signup form or a post-signup survey: "How long have you been watching my channel?" Or if you're doing sales calls, you just ask them on it. And then once you start collecting this data, you'll see patterns. It might say people have been watching for six months, which is great—it means they're sticking around and watching you.
But if people aren't saying a few weeks or a few days, it might mean you're missing out on a lot of sales.
Now, I have this data on Rev Track, which tells me how long it's taken from the first time someone clicked on the link below one of my videos to how long it took them to convert to that offer, which is the next best thing for working this out.
Step 2
Now we move on to Step Two, which is the foundation you have to master—otherwise you can't convert viewers to buyers. And it works like this:
So in order to get as many people buying from you, you need to get everyone who discovers you to watch about 10 to 20 videos in their first 30 days. That's the kind of average I'm working on here. Now, that might sound like a lot, but it's actually pretty simple if you think of it like this—and your own behavior when you find a really high-quality channel.
So to start, viewers might find one video, love it, then they binge five more on day one. So that's six. Then you release four more across the month—so one a week—meaning they come back each week. That's given us 10. And then if every video from that month ends strong, they'll end clicking your end screen and hopefully watch another one. So we might get another four out of them. That's like 13–14 videos in total in 30 days—which doesn't sound that hard when you think of it like that, right?
Now, the thing is, in order to get people to watch that many in that time frame, you've got to make sure your YouTube channel looks like this pie.
So imagine you love apple pie. Every time you see a slice, you just devour it. But the rest of this pie—one slice is cherry, the other's rhubarb, the other's banana—all flavors you hate. So you take a bite, then you spit it out and you go, "Oh, I'm not coming back to that pie—too risky, not of interest."
That's how viewers feel when you have a channel that's full of unrelated content that isn't about the problem they originally discovered you by.
So if one video you make is about confidence, the next is about relationships, then the next is about communication—each one solves a totally different problem. And that breaks the system.
Because if only two out of 10 videos interested them, it could take 5 to 6 months to hit that 10–20 videos consumed that we really want to be aiming for—and most people just won't stick around that long.
But worse: if your videos start solving problems your hottest viewers don't care about anymore, they stop clicking altogether. And YouTube will just stop recommending them. So your channel gets no views, no sales—and kind of fails.
But if every video solves the same problem for the same person, then every slice of your channel is Apple. Meaning no matter what YouTube shows them, it's the perfect fit every time.
This increases our chances of getting them to watch those 10 or 20 videos in the first month—so they'll buy from you, if you follow the next few steps I'm going to show you too.
But the second thing that really matters is: if you want to create a profitable channel, you need to understand your offer is a pie too—and it needs to match your channel, slice for slice.
So if your channel is helping people with relationships, but your offer is about confidence, your viewer lands on your sales page and thinks, "Came here for apple—this fool's giving me bananas. I'm off."
And it doesn't matter how good your product is—if it doesn't feel relevant to your viewers, it ain't going to sell.
So here's the fix: you make sure your channel and your offer both solve the same problem for the same person. Then every video you post warms the viewer up for sales.
And I'm going to link a video at the end that's going to show you how to do this in way more detail.
Step 3
So Step 3 is to set up a flywheel that gets viewers buying in that first 30-day period. And a flywheel is just a content structure that makes this really easy to do. And it's made up of three parts.
The first is a discoverable video, the second is a trust builder, and the third is a pitch video. And each of them has a role.
So let's say you run a weight loss coaching business. A discoverable video might be "How I Lost 20kg Without Running or Cardio," and it gets tons of clicks and reaches people cold.
Then a trust builder might be a video titled "Here's What I Eat in a Day to Stay in Shape in My 30s" — not as viral, but it's the type of thing someone binge-watches after the first video because they like the way you do things.
And then the pitch video is where you just introduce your product or service in a really value-focused, non-sleazy way. Now, all of these have to have call-to-actions in the description or in the video itself that point to your product or service, and that's really important.
Now if you do this right, you're going to find that when someone lands on your channel, they watch one, then two, then three videos, and by the third video — boom — they're on your email list or they're booking a call, and you're converting cold traffic into warm leads.
Now you can create multiple flywheels to test which videos convert the best, but even if you just have one solid flywheel, you're going to see an increase in conversions almost immediately.
Step 4
Step 4 is to increase the number of people who enter the flywheel.
So now that we have a system that gets people from viewer to buyer, we want to get more of them in that system — and that means making your discoverable videos more discoverable.
To do that, you need to improve the two things that get you more views: your click-through rate and your average view duration.
The first one, click-through rate, is the percentage of people who see your thumbnail and title and decide to click on your video.
So if your click-through rate is 4%, that means for every 100 people who see your video on their homepage or in search results, only four of them click.
We want to increase that — ideally to 8, 10, or even 12% if we can.
Now, to do that, you need to test a ton of different thumbnail and title styles. And I like to use the SHIT framework to do this — Story, Hook, Image, Text — and make sure every thumbnail has these four elements.
Then once you get someone to click, the next thing that matters is keeping them watching. That's your average view duration.
So if your video is 10 minutes long, and your average view duration is only two minutes, that tells YouTube people aren't sticking around — and it won't recommend that video as much.
But if you can get it up to five, six, seven minutes — YouTube goes, "Oh wow, people love this," and starts promoting it more.
So what I want you to do is pick your best-performing discoverable video — the one with the most views — and look at its click-through rate and average view duration.
Then try three new thumbnail/title combos and see which performs best.
And then watch the video yourself and see where the drop-off points are. Are you taking too long to get to the point? Is the hook weak? Is there a visual lull that could be improved?
This kind of analysis — yes, it takes time — but it's the difference between a flywheel that works and one that dies on the runway.
Step 5
Step 5 is to use your pitch video to build an email list — not just drive direct sales.
Because here's the truth: not everyone is going to buy right away. In fact, most won't.
But if you get them on your email list, you now have the ability to follow up, build a relationship, and convert them later — even months down the line.
Now, to do this, you need a lead magnet. That's just something valuable you give away in exchange for an email.
Could be a PDF, a free course, a quiz — whatever fits your niche and helps them move closer to solving their problem.
Then, in your pitch video, you mention the lead magnet and put the link in the description and comments. Simple as that.
But here's what most people get wrong: they treat the lead magnet as a side thing. They mention it once and forget about it.
What you want to do is treat it like a mini-offer. Make it sound exciting. Show a quick demo of what's inside. Talk about the results people get from using it.
If you do that, you'll see way more email signups — and that turns into way more sales over time.
Step 6
Step 6 is to optimize your flywheel using actual buyer feedback.
So once people start buying — from your videos, from your lead magnet, from your emails — you want to ask them: "What was the moment you knew you wanted to buy?"
You can do this in an email, on a call, in a survey — doesn't matter how.
But the answer to that question is gold.
Because if they say, "It was when you said XYZ in that video," or "It was when I read that one sentence on your landing page," — boom — you now know what's converting, and you can double down on it.
You can highlight that moment earlier in your videos. You can turn it into a headline. You can build a whole new video around that exact concept.
This is how you go from just making content… to making content that sells.
And if you want me to break down the exact pitch video structure I use that gets people onto my email list and into my products without sounding pushy, I've got a full video walking through it — just click right here.

## Video 4 · The EXACT Storytelling System That Turns Viewers In To Buyers

Why is it that most people struggle to make money from YouTube videos even though they do everything you're taught — like making funnels and using ads — whilst other people like Dan Co and Brian Johnson and myself can generate millions from the platform?
Well, it's because they ignore the most powerful tool on the planet for views and sales and leads — where all you need to do is just tell three different types of stories and you'll turn viewers into buyers like nothing else you've seen.
So in this video I'm going to give you the exact storytelling system I've used on multiple channels for years that turns viewers into buyers really fast. In fact, this thing is so powerful I used to use it to sell out a $5,000 program every time — with no sales calls, no email nurture, no ads, no webinar, no real funnel at all — other than doing what I'm about to show you, which is almost unheard of.
All right, so for this storytelling system to work you have to understand first why...
Why Tell Stories
Telling stories is so powerful. What I'm going to do is deliver the same information now in two different ways and then you can make your own mind up about if you bother watching the rest of the video based on it.
Okay, so I've used this first story before in a video — you might have heard it. If you have, just stick with it and I'll explain all the power behind it later.
Option 1: Story 1
Number one — I discovered this YouTube tack about 20 years ago when I had a lawn care business, and I just spilled some fertilizer that stains really badly.
Now I cleaned it up as fast as I could, got rid of the stain, but the client started souring at me saying she just spent £50,000 on the drive and the stain was still there — and that if I couldn't fix it, I was going to have to pay for it. And I was only 18 — I didn't have any money.
The thing is, the stain had gone. But she insisted it was there and that I was going to have to pay to fix it. And I was getting really angry because she was just lying.
So I left.
Two weeks later I was working on a neighbor's lawn and she came over and she said, "Oh Ed, the stain's gone, and look — sorry I was so hotheaded the other day. My son died in a car crash that morning."
And my anger towards this woman just vanished in an instant. And that is when I realized that sometimes people lash out just because they're having a bad day.
So don't fight the people who troll you in the comments — because you just don't know what they might be going through in their own life.
All right, so that was option number one.
Option 2: Story 2
Now option number two — I'm going to make my point like this.
Okay, so this next YouTube hack is to not respond to the trolls in the comments 'cause they could be having a bad day, so just move on.
Can you feel the difference between the two? Yes, version one was longer — but version two, that's not going to make you go, "You know what, it's right. Maybe I should let go."
Because you can feel my old client's pain when I tell that story. And you just empathize with her so much. And I can pretty much guarantee that story will stick with you because of that — especially the next time you read a nasty comment.
So the reason storytelling is so powerful is because people don't remember what you said — they remember how you made them feel.
So if we look at people like Brian Johnson for example — the man who's trying to inch backwards — he really stirs up a lot of feelings in people. Now some hate him for what he's trying to do, defying death, and others love him for it. But you cannot deny he makes people feel something. And it's a big, big reason for his traction.
But you're probably not looking to go front page of the global press with your business. So just consider this: if your content bores people, they remember that. But if you can give them some kind of emotional reaction, they're going to connect it to your channel and your business. And this is how you build lifelong fans that come back and buy your stuff — without the need for really complex marketing.
But that's not all telling stories is going to do for your sales and your business and your channel. There's a few more reasons it's going to generate you sales.
So the first one is this — and if you ever want to land a dream client, I highly recommend doing this. So check this out:
In this video, I told a story about how I'd gotten really ill and I was fed up, and I thought, "I'm going to retire. Can't do this anymore." And then I started planning my retirement and realized that I basically had nothing else to do. And retirement is a miserable existence.
So I planned out what my dream life would look like — and it basically looked like making YouTubes and helping people grow businesses. So I realized, "Well, you're basically retired now then. So if money doesn't matter to you anymore, get rid of everything you hate doing and just do what you love instead."
Now that — in terms of stories — is a pretty unrelatable problem for a lot of you. But I got emails and WhatsApps from very big influencers going, "Ed, this is exactly what I'm going through. Can we have a chat? Because it sounds like you figured out the answer to my biggest problem right now?"
And then this video from a few years ago I made about how I deleted all of my old videos on my channel because I thought they were bringing in the wrong audience and it was holding my channel back — and I made this video about it.
And you wouldn't believe it, but all the calls that got booked in after making that were from people who had been wondering the exact same thing.
And then I made this video here where I talk about niching down and quitting my old channel — and guess what? The business that comes in is from people who are thinking about doing the same thing.
So the next reason stories are just so powerful for your business is because they — firstly — they raise your credibility a lot by showing people you've solved the problem they have — like in that moment, right now.
But secondly, they're highly relatable and relevant because anyone going through what your story proves you've solved is going to go, "Oh wait — this is happening to me." And that's going to hook them like nothing else and make them more likely to want to work with you when they see the story you tell about how you solved it.
On top of this — stories get people lining up to work with you because they simplify complex ideas and they make it very easy for people to learn — meaning they have those lightbulb moments, which blow up your sales.
And then there's the fact that by telling stories about the problem viewers have, you inspire them to take action.
So just think about how powerful a viewer who finally believes in themself is — versus an uninspired one — for your sales.
Copy What Works
This is probably my favorite thing about stories.
So if I were to say to you, "You can have a Rolex, or you can have a fake Rolex — which one do you want?"
YouTube is this big game of copy-what-works. So what you see all the time is this — now I'm not having a go at these people, please don't go commenting on their videos — they're doing what you're taught, kind of copy-what-works.
But if you want to be the Rolex in your niche, you don't do it copying other people in such an exact way like this.
Instead what you want to do is become Cartier. So you take what works, then make it your own — so it doesn't look like a copy, but a slightly different version. Because at the end of the day, Cartier and Rolex — they make the same thing. But it still feels very different the way they do it, right?
So because there's already so much information online, it's very unlikely that everything you say will be unique to your channel.
So the way you get around this — because sometimes we just copy by mistake — is by using unique stories that make your point. And you will have hundreds of them from your entire life. I mean, I just pulled a story out from 20 years ago when I stained someone's drive.
But I'm telling you now — you're going to remember that one.
All right, so now you know why you must not sleep on them if you want to make easier sales and you want to build viewership.
Let me show you how to easily get into storytelling without it taking up all your time.
So what most people think is storytelling is all about making a video like this — with a big epic story from start to finish and it's got music, it's got B-roll, it's got tons going on.
Now those are great and I used to spend weeks working on them, but it's not really sustainable to spend weeks full-time on YouTube videos and trying to run a business. And the sustainability and the consistency is how you're going to grow the business.
So all I do now is tell short stories in my videos to help make a point — like I've already done a few times in this video.
So what you want to do is start small — and I actually don't think you ever have to go much further than what I've been doing.
And that's kind of it.
So before I break down the three formats that can make you millions — and all of the biggest personal brands out there, they swear by these things — you need to understand the things...
The Circle
...that you have to include to turn your story into an unforgettable piece of content that grows your business.
And that's the thing that makes this tricky — because people always think telling a story is what's powerful.
It's not.
Telling a good one is.
And that is the difference between Green Lantern and Iron Man.
Okay, so the best way to explain what makes a story that will create fans and return viewers and buyers is like this circle. You have the beginning at the top here, and you have the end down here.
Now all you need to do is ask yourself:
What's the problem the hero wants to solve at the start?
And then what happens here at the end?
So in the best movie of all time — Wicked — that I've not seen three times in the theater and multiple times on TV and never cried when Defying Gravity kicks in... Elphaba starts off where everyone kind of doesn't like her 'cause she's green and she's misunderstood and she's dreaming of the Wizard.
And then the movie ends where she realizes, oh, she's actually okay with being hated by everyone so long as she stays true to her values — and she's now anti-Wizard 'cause it turns out he was a bit of a dick all along.
Or you got Rocky, which starts here with the belief that everyone thinks he's a bum and he's got really low self-esteem — but at the end, even though he loses, he proves his worth to himself and he gains the respect of lots of other people.
So if we relate this to more everyday lessons — not like an epic musical that you really need to watch — your story could start with the problem that you're working, say, 12 hours a day and you're feeling like growth requires burnout and you have this belief that more effort equals more results.
Then in the end of your story, you're working less, you're earning more, and you're finally feeling free. And your new belief is: focus, systems, and leverage equals more impact with less time. So you now only work 4 hours a day.
So a great story is just about making sure your ending puts the person it's about in a very different position to where they started.
It doesn't actually matter if you succeed or you fail in this quest you go on — you just need to show some sort of lesson or transformation.
So my story about the drive started with the problem: me potentially getting sued with a very angry client that I was kind of deep down hating for the lies she was spreading about me.
And it ended with me feeling the utmost empathy for her — and kind of feeling bad about myself because I'd not taken the time to consider the real reason for her stress.
So my transformation here is from rage to empathy. And that's what makes that one a good story despite the topic of it and how sad it is.
The next thing that makes a story really flipping powerful and really memorable — it's not that easy to find — but it's why everyone remembers The Sixth Sense, Fight Club, The Usual Suspects, The Prestige.
Because they just totally blindside you with an epic twist at the end that you didn't expect.
And that's what makes my driveway story powerful again — unless you've heard me tell it in my email or my older videos, there's no possible way you could have called that ending.
So the ultimate story is when the ending has the most unexpected outcome too.
I mean, you're not sitting there going, "Oh my gosh Ed, you spilled fertilizer are you okay?"
You're going: that poor woman — and you remember that lesson as a result.
And then after that you just need to tick off a few more things, which I've kind of simplified down to the very foundations.
So the first works like this:
Let’s say you’re teaching people how to make money by streaming music on Spotify. And to make your point, you tell the story of how Elon Musk makes decisions to run his 700 companies.
That point might be relevant… but what you want to do is ask yourself — is Musk really that relatable to my viewers, who are probably struggling to get one business off the ground?
So although inspiring and maybe interesting, I think you want to make sure your story is incredibly relatable to your viewer’s own situation, where they are now.
So if you started with the problem your viewers have now — about how you kept trying different ways to make money on Spotify by posting your music in random forums — and at the end you realize you only need to do one simple, unexpected thing that was a lot simpler to get results…
You would have told a relatable story that went from where the viewer is now — confused and struggling — to thriving.
And they dig that way more.
Dig that. 1970s here.
Also — remember what I said about how you don’t want to be a fake Rolex? Well, anyone can tell the Musk story. So to differentiate you, you’re just better off using your own unique stories all of the time.
Now the next thing that makes a great story that turns viewers into buyers is to embrace how much you sucked — by telling stories that focus on your pain and problems.
Never worry that you are openly saying you sucked at the thing your video is teaching — because people just gravitate to those who show vulnerability, as well as people who are relatable.
But also, nobody likes the story of the rich person who had the easiest life, where everything was amazing and just kept getting richer.
There’s no lesson. There’s no transformation. It’s not relatable.
So this isn’t about showing off.
In fact, I’m kind of at this weird point where when something really bad happens in my business, I’m like, “Yes! This disgusting pain that’s keeping me up at night will eventually turn into a fantastic story when I can fix it.”
And the final thing from the foundations — that’s critical you do not blow — I learned this when I was making this video about split-testing thumbnails.
So I basically did a challenge where I updated lots of old videos. And I learned like a hundred different things. I was really excited to make this deep-dive story all about it. I had data on click rates and thumbnail psychology and I was writing this and realized:
“Wait a second — this is such a bad story.”
It’s so complex. It’s interesting to me, at my level… but to most people? They’re going to zone out.
So I stripped it back to just one lesson I learned and built the entire story around that.
So the key to storytelling is to keep things simple.
And that brings us on to the middle of this circle, where all you do to make your story really slap (which is a good thing apparently) is show the conflict you came up against whilst trying to get from here to here.
So our demo channel might have tried all kinds of productivity hacks and kept piling on more work and was getting really fed up. So they eventually decided to stop doing any of them and to strip it back to just one hack they mastered — and that’s what worked.
So really, your circle just looks like this:
Start with a problem and the relatable pain your viewers have too
Show the struggle and how you went on to solve it
End on the transformation
Then summarize the lesson
That’s it. That’s not too hard, right?
And here’s one really important mistake you need to avoid when it does come to writing or telling this:
Avoid This
You need to avoid saying things like this:
“I was drinking a cup of tea on a Wednesday afternoon with Jeremy…”
…who then left and plays no part in the rest of the story.
If you have a line in your story and you can delete it and it doesn’t impact the end — you must get rid of it.
Otherwise your viewers are going to be like, “Get to the point.”
So you would delete the fact that you were drinking a cup of tea on a Wednesday afternoon with Jeremy.
So now you know why story matters, the foundations, and how to plan one.
I’m going to give you the three frameworks I’ve used for years now — that blew up my business, built up my sales — and I’m confident they will do too for you if you get it right.
And I want you to...
Act 1, 2, 3
...imagine these three stories are told at three different acts in your own journey.
Act One — this separates you from everyone else in your niche. It builds credibility, it generates sales, makes you more unique.
Act Two will take that to the next level, and you’ll see your sales just massively increase if you use this well.
And Act Three is — I’d say — maybe the most powerful. As you don’t just separate yourself from the niche… you lead it, in a way where everyone who discovers you goes: “This person is on a different level to everyone else.”
And that’s my own personal goal with this channel. And I’m going to show you my plan to do that as well — to help you work out your own.
Act 1 Storytelling
Without this, generating sales and leads is just not going to happen. And if you don’t have many clients yet, or you’re newer to your niche or business, then it will pretty much be the main thing you have to rely on to get any traction at all.
And it works like this.
So years ago I was trolled a lot for talking about YouTube, ’cause I had a few thousand subscribers. And I mean — that’s fair. Why would you listen to someone talking about something their channel didn’t look like it had done yet, right?
So I was like, well I need to prove I’m legit.
So I told a story about how I monetized a channel in 6 weeks — which back then was like unheard of — and boom: millions of views. And suddenly I was less of a joke.
But this isn’t about virality. What I really want to show you is just the power of dropping stories into your videos that give proof you can get a result, so that people watch it and go:
"Oh yeah, this person’s legit."
And that will help to turn your views into sales.
So Act 1 storytelling is just telling stories about how you solved problems your viewers have too.
And I know it sounds kind of overblown, but if you can’t do that, I’m not sure how you can make it now.
Now — if you’re like a doctor and your channel is about fixing bad backs and you’ve never had one — that’s okay. You’re an Act 2 channel by default, so you don’t have to worry too much about that.
What you really want to do though is try to include one story of this nature in every video. Because if someone binges five videos in a row — by the end of that session, they’re going to go:
"This person’s clearly an expert. I’m coming back. In fact… I should be paying for this stuff. Wait — I wonder if I can pay them to help me?"
And that brings us on to Act 2 Storytelling.
And again — you want to try and use this as often as you can, if it is possible.
Because years ago, even though I was getting traction on my channel, I was making these videos that show people how I was blowing up.
And I always used to get comments going,
"Well if you’re so flipping good, do it to someone else’s channel."
And I was like, “Ohhh, it’s a guy you’ve got to do to impress you guys.”
But I took it on board, and I started making videos about how I blow up other people’s channels and businesses too.
And this was massive for sales.
Because when you’re marketing, people — they go,
"Yeah it works for you, but will it work for me?"
So if you show someone like your viewer, with their problem, getting the result they want with your system, it’s like:
"Oh. This channel is good for me."
And you go from:
“This person’s learning on the go, reporting back,”
to
“This person’s an expert helping others.”
And that causes a very different reaction.
So this is why you need to use your client wins to make your point.
Now — if you don’t have clients, but you’ve helped someone else get a result and they didn’t pay you — that’s fine. You can say that too.
And then we move on to Act 3 Storytelling.
This is the act which I believe we should all strive to be in.
It’s not going to be easy — but it’s my ultimate goal.
And that is to make videos where people go:
“This person is on another level to everyone else in their niche.”
Now if you can create this reaction, your competition doesn’t exist when it comes to someone making a sales decision.
Your viewers are just going to go:
"I just want to work with this person over everyone else."
And this way of thinking is helping this channel make millions.
And it also gets people like Dan Co and Brian Johnson mega fans.
So let me show you how this works.
If we break down what these acts really are:
Act One is your past — it’s how you solved the problem.
Act Two is the present — it’s how you’re solving your clients’ problems.
Act Three is how you’re shaping the future.
So what happens is, as you get more and more traction with your stories, your niche is going to start to copy you. And they might even start telling their own powerful stories.
But this is where you actively try to shape the entire future of your whole niche.
So let me give you an example.
I quit my channel with almost 400,000 subscribers because I thought there was a better way for businesses to do this.
And to help me establish this new way, I built a software that tracks leads from videos.
And as a result of this, my entire view of YouTube has completely shifted — meaning I could now give content to you guys that’s not on Google anywhere — because nobody else has this data.
But not only that — it enabled me to build AI and new systems for my clients that you just can’t find anywhere else in the world, which helps us level up as one big community.
Now Brian Johnson — love him or hate him — he’s a perfect example of the power of this too.
So his backstory is he made millions in tech, and now he’s testing all this anti-aging stuff on himself.
And he’s got this insane world-changing vision which his fans are totally bought into.
Which means he’s living in Act Three.
And that’s why he gets so much press.
He’s built like a cult following.
And then there’s Dan Co — he’s building new AI to try and change the world of being a creator — and he documents it too.
So Act Three is about trying to take your niche to new levels that have not been explored yet.
And in an ideal world, you build something kind of publicly, and then report back going:
“Hey people — this is amazing. And here’s the amount of proof I have that it works. And this is the better way. And it’s going to change your life.”
And to do that, you just tell stories of what you’re doing — in the same way — using the same circle framework.
But like I said, this not one for the faint-hearted.
Because one — you might have to go to extremes where you devote your entire life to it. So kiss goodbye to the 4-hour work week.
But also — you build in public, and things don’t work out.
So you might have to one day publicly go:
“Do you know what? My software didn’t work. No one bought it. It was a failure.”
But guess what?
If that happens, you just have another painful story to share.
So you can’t lose.
And if you want to start practicing storytelling, you can do it in a very simple video that keeps blowing up channels — and I’ve turned it into a very simple step-by-step process.
Will take you 8 minutes to watch.
And you can discover it in this video here.
And from the people who’ve already seen it — they’ve already commented saying it blew up their channels.

## Video 5 · The EXACT System to make $10k From Youtube in 7 Days

Intro Summary
In 2023 I helped 40 people launch a simple product to their YouTube audience and the average one made $10,000 in its first seven days of its existence. Since then I've helped over 100 people launch them. And although we don't always win big, most of the time we succeed. So today I'm going to give you the proven system we used to make these channels an average of 10K in 7 days so you can start earning more.
First though—how did they make $10,000 in 7 days? That sounds like utter internet marketing talk.
Results
Here's the raw transparency of it. So the 40 people I tested this product launch system on—they all had different size audiences and they were in a ton of different niches. So we had one channel getting about 1,000 views per video who made $6,000 in the first seven days. We had a channel getting 20,000 views per video who made not far off like $50,000 I think it was.
And what happened was two of the 40 test users who launched made under $2,000. And since then a lot more people have done this. And about 20% have these kind of mind-blowing results that shock us in a good way. 50% get like a good result. 10% bad because things can go wrong. But don't worry—I'm going to show you how to deal with that later and why it doesn't really mean that much if they do go wrong. And then the final 20%—maybe like less than hoped for.
So this isn't like a guaranteed magic internet money printer. But it can be very successful so long as you stick to the plan that I'm going to lay out—and of course you have some sort of following you can promote it to.
How Many Viewers
But how many viewers do you need to make $10,000 in the first seven days for launching? That's the thing. It's going to be very different based on your own situation and your niche.
So if I was just to give a really rough benchmark—it’s 3,000 views per video consistently and at least 1,000 people on your email list. However for some people that's going to make a grand. Others it could make 50.
Now you might be going "Great, I'm miles off of that." But check this out. So this is Air. He's not a client. He sent me his Rev track data that shows link clicks and sales from YouTube—link below. And look, he's making sales from videos.
But this is the really cool thing. His channel at the time he submitted this had under 900 subscribers—but also his product wasn't actually finished. It was in beta. And he got people to buy it to test it for him.
And look—it started selling. So it might not be 10K in 7 days—but it’s a start.
However there is another option here. So if you have a high ticket offer or service, you can do what Liam's been doing recently.
So he made over $600,000 in 90 days using ads and his YouTube channel. But he also built a similar low-ticket option like I’m going to show you, but he uses it to upsell to a larger offer. And that’s helped generate $53,000 in one month just from his channel’s organic views—getting around like 1,000 to 2,000 per video.
What is this product
So what is this product? Well I call it a workshop or a masterass. You can do them live then sell them on demand, or just make them on demand and have no live.
Now the critical thing to note here is—this is not a course. I'm going to go into why courses are just a really bad idea later on.
But think of it like a paid video deep dive. Which I know sounds like nobody’s ever going to buy because you do them for free on your channel, right? But my first one years ago made $80,000 over its life. The second one—$140,000. Viewers love them.
And what makes these even more powerful is—they’re really easy to make. And on top of that, they can work in so many niches.
So Brian here—he helps people make Star Wars theme rooms. And we helped him launch a workshop and it did amazing. This was from the Google business space.
Basically—if you help people solve a problem or improve something, this can work for you. And it's a flipping great place to start.
Oh, and by the way—if you want to see the workshop I made that hit $80,000, I’ll give it to you for free in the link below. But please, please, please keep in mind—I made this thing years ago. It is not representative of my paid products or program now. All right? It was the first thing I ever did.
Course vs Workshop
Okay—so before we move on to the system to make these, there’s one big mistake that is critical you avoid. And that is building a course, not a workshop or a masterass like I show you.
So let me explain why you really have to avoid this.
So if I asked you to build me a skyscraper, could you do it? Someone watching is probably going to be like "Yeah I do that for a living actually." But for most of us—I mean, where do you start? The bricks? The foundation? The plan for the plumbing? The electrics?
You're going to get that very wrong unless you have a ton of experience. But if I said to you—"Can you build me a garden shed?" You'd go, "Yeah of course." Because it’s basically just four walls nailed together with a roof on top.
That is the difference between a course and one of these workshops.
If you try to build a massive course that covers everything, and you’ve never made an info product before, and you don’t know how to plan it, or how to simplify, or how to teach—you’re going to spend months working on something that is...
And this is also why the course industry has such a bad name—because everyone goes, "I’m going to make a giant course," which is very hard to make.
And whatever you do—do not fall into the trap of thinking your viewers would only want a skyscraper. That’s not true. Because if they did, nobody would have a garden shed. We’d just have these giant buildings in our gardens blocking out all the light.
The whole idea of a workshop is you target one specific problem and help people overcome it in a simple way—like a garden shed does to keep your lawn mower dry.
And Lord help us if you make a giant course and charge $30 for it.
We’re going to go into pricing later—but that is like the ultimate disaster fee.
Now I should probably say—I’m not saying don’t eventually make something larger. But more that if you’ve not done anything like this before, you got to keep it simple.
The Simple System
Okay so now let's get into the simple system to produce and launch them. And then I'm going to show you what to do if your sales don't work out and how to deal with selling to your audience in a way that they're actually going to really like.
Okay so step one is simply about picking something to cover that's going to make you money.
All right so let's go back to my first ever workshop, which you can get in the link below. That is no representation of my current offer—and ask, why did you pick making thumbnails to talk about?
Well the reason I did this was because I used to take one-to-one calls with viewers and I noticed nobody really seemed to understand them. So I just thought, I'm going to make a workshop that helps them with that.
Was it the best idea? No, not really. Did it make money? Yeah it did.
So although there is a research process I put my clients through now to work all of this out—if you want to keep it simple, just ask yourself:
What's the video topic that always gets views for me?
What's the problem I see people always talking about?
And that’s a pretty good place to start.
Now what you must not do—ever, ever—is go, “I want to talk about X.”
It has to be:
What do my viewers want to know about?
What’s the specific problem I could help them solve?
Step two—everyone gets this wrong—because they go and make the product.
Do not do that.
Instead, what you need to do is this:
So when I came up with a test product that helped people launch these workshops, the first thing I did was I asked myself:
“What could I do or say in my marketing that would make this impossible for people to say no to?”
So I just wrote down this really big claim, which was:
“If you follow every single step, I guarantee you will make more than this thing costs—otherwise I'll pay you back the difference.”
And I thought, Damn, that's good.
And the cool thing is—it worked.
I only refunded one person $500. And the other person who didn’t quite make their money back? They didn’t want any money back because they had such a good experience.
So that’s the next step:
You need to come up with this big claim that you can help someone get the result they want—so it’s very hard for them to say no to.
Now a word of warning here:
For a workshop that doesn't have feedback and support, it's probably not advised you go that big with your claim like I did on the product I mentioned—because the guarantee made it very clear that they had to submit literally every single thing they did for feedback.
It was a complete deal breaker if they missed one thing.
So if you can’t actually check what your buyers are doing—which you probably can’t with a master class or workshop—what you want to do is make sure that your claim doesn’t set unrealistic expectations… but is still very appealing.
So think to yourself:
What is a realistic result I could help them get?
And that might be something like:
“My system will help you grow the perfect tomatoes every time.”
“This will help you master the three scales for the perfect solo.”
“This will help you knit the highest quality winter scarf.”
Basically, you come up with the result… and then run a quick test to make sure you can actually get them this result.
And that test works like this:
So when I came up with the “you can't lose” claim, I thought:
That’s amazing. Is that even possible?
So I thought about—well hang on—what would you have to do to get someone who’s never made a product before a result?
Because they’re going to suck at everything.
So I thought—
Well I could maybe write the sales page for them as a template, and then have them tweak it to their own words.
And then I could maybe come up with a sales video format they could use in the same way.
And then I could give them seven emails to promote their launch that I would just write for them, and they could just adjust.
And I basically realized—
Oh I can pretty much do most of the work for them.
They just need to follow a big list of instructions.
And then I thought—
You know what, if I do that, I feel pretty confident I can make that claim.
So at this stage, you just work out—without planning everything in depth—it’s just like an overview:
Can I actually deliver on the claim?
So think about templates or to-do lists or even helping them use AI to get a result.
So a claim might sound like this:
“My workshop will give you the exact system I use to create a million-dollar sales page that turns viewers into buyers.”
And then you just give them the templates and training on how to build their own.
So it’s all about the specific result, not the “ultimate guide to everything.”
Then we move on to the next step which is still not making it.
And what we want to do is we want to give ourselves time to promote it before you sell so that you can actually get some strong sales on launch.
So remember Amit from earlier—he was driving people to a waitlist to learn when it might open. So it makes sense to do the same as you build this thing.
So what you could do is—you can start to tease that something is coming in your videos and your community wall and your email, and then make a little form that gets people to join the waitlist.
The idea being—anyone on this waitlist might get some sort of special discount when it opens.
And then what you do is—let's say it takes you 30 days to make this product. Not all day every day—you’re just fitting it around your life. Once a week you put out some little teaser, some little hype post to get people excited and get them onto the waitlist.
Or you can do what Amir did and just mention it in the first 90 seconds of your video—and look how many clicks he got from his viewers.
People were actually interested.
And then after that the next step is—we still don’t make it.
What you want to start doing now is—you want to build out your sales page and the promotion that you're going to do around it.
Now sales pages—they're a whole video on their own. So I’m just going to give you three things yours really needs to include.
All right—so the first is a headline and a sub-headline.
Now your headline—it needs to sum up your offer in a short sentence and make someone say:
"This sounds like exactly what I need."
With the idea being—they then want to read the sub-headline to find out more.
Now—spend the time on the headline. If it takes you as long to come up with a headline and sub-headline as it does the rest of the page—that's pretty normal.
Do not rush this. It's a hook.
So all it needs to do is just set up a big problem and a specific result in a short sentence. Now some of the best headlines will give a time frame too. But just make sure you keep it problem and result specific.
So for example:
“Plan, cook, and batch produce the perfect six-pack meal prep in only 60 minutes a week.”
Then your sub-headline just has to fill in the gaps of the headline. It gets a bit more specific.
Now—you really want to make sure this emphasizes benefits over features.
So for example:
“A step-by-step masterass that will give you all the ingredients, recipes and tricks you need to quickly batch produce an entire week's worth of healthy meals.”
So you can see that just after reading these two lines the viewer’s going to go:
"Okay cool. I get the value in this. Sounds like the sort of thing I'm down with."
Now the next thing you want to include in your page is going to harness what your viewers love the most about you—and that is you.
Because you pop a video on that page that does three very simple things:
Sets up the problem the viewers have
Sets up the solution to the problem
Says the workshop will solve the problem—
...and then you just ask them to buy it.
You can keep these things really simple on a low-ticket workshop.
Now the next thing you need to do is just make sure the rest of the copy on your page is always benefits over features.
So many people list things on their sales page like “this includes PDFs and templates” and the viewers are not going, “Oh my gosh PDFs, oh my problems are over.”
So a general rule of thumb here is—if you ever list a headline on your site or want to sell something, make sure it’s about the benefit it brings, not the thing you’re selling.
So that would look like this:
“Keep track of your calories and lose weight faster.”
And then under that you'd have something like:
“My easy-to-use diet tracker will stop you binging on food that wrecks your weight loss.”
And it’s just a PDF.
You basically say it’s the solution to the problem you think they have.
Now do you want to know something crazy?
If you get really good at making videos—you don’t really need a huge amount more on these pages.
Because YouTube videos warm people up like crazy.
So just make sure you get in those foundational things—and do your best on your content.
And this is going to be a really nice simple way to start.
How To Get Fast Sales
All right—so the next step, where we still don’t make the thing—we now need to work out how we’re going to get fast sales on this.
So one of the things about low-ticket products like this is: your sales really explode when there is a reason to buy right now—not tomorrow.
So if you want to hit $10,000 in 7 days, you need to harness urgency.
So here’s a few ways you can do it so your buyers don’t dilly-dally:
Now the first is—if you do this live—well, they kind of have to buy the ticket, right? So that creates urgency. There’s a countdown, naturally.
But if you’re not doing it live—or even if you are—the second way to create urgency is just to do like a limited space discount. So say:
“Only 20 at this price remain.”
And then the third option you have is just a time discount. So you could say:
“The discount remains until the following day.”
Now the next thing you can do is a value add, where you say:
“Anyone who signs up before this date gets something extra.”
So I used to add a free studio build course to one of my courses, and it used to just blow up sales every time I added it. And it meant I didn’t have to discount.
Now one easy thing to value add is you could say:
“Anyone who buys will get an invite to a live group Q&A session after the workshop.”
So you can watch the master class and then ask questions about anything you’re stuck on—which I used before and it worked really well.
Now you just want something for the launch that makes it feel like a better deal if they get in there now.
Pricing
So now let’s look at pricing.
What do you charge?
Well—you have to avoid the trap you are almost guaranteed to fall into. Because we all do. I’ve been there.
So years ago, we were going to launch this Netflix-style product. It was like a membership subscription with a ton of content. It was really slick.
And when we looked at all the competition—everyone was charging like $9.99 a month.
So to hit $10,000 a month—which was kind of like our initial goal—we’d have to sell a thousand of them.
And I turned around, I was like:
“This is dumb. Like—we’re going to have to play such a volume game. We need to charge more.”
The person I was helping said:
“I can’t charge more. Why would anyone spend more when everyone else is charging $9.99?”
But I said:
“No. We got to go in at like $50 to $60 a month. Just forget what everyone else is doing.
Viewers don’t shop around. They just buy from the creator they like.”
So we went ahead with that.
And it ended up generating hundreds of thousands of dollars.
And about a year later—someone with a massive app that used to charge $9.99 a month for it, who had 10,000 users, told me they were losing money—as the cost of managing it required so much people and tech setup that it was getting too expensive.
So they had more people emailing, more people to manage… less money.
Whereas our system—no management at all, more money.
So I’m not saying charge $59 or whatever. But what you need to do is:
Charge on the value your offer brings.
And do not worry about all the other people who are going to be massively undervaluing themselves in your niche.
They won’t be earning enough. And they probably have no idea what they’re doing.
So as a ballpark—I always say:
Never charge less than $49 for one of these workshops. No matter what it is.
But you got to ask yourself:
If someone follows this advice, how much would that be worth to them?
So—if you help people get out of debt—that could be worth $50,000 to them. So paying $250 is a bargain.
If you help people learn three songs on the guitar to a level they could pick it up and play at their daughter’s wedding as she comes down the aisle—that is priceless.
And that’s the thing—you just don’t know how much someone might value what you’re going to show them.
But whatever you do—just don’t make this cheap because you think your viewers don’t have any money.
That is not true.
That is your own limiting belief that you need to work on. And could just be a reflection of your own kind of like financial situation right now.
But then—this is the other thing about pricing:
You just don’t get it right straight away.
So what you’re going to do is:
You’re gonna find the right price.
So for example—one of my clients said:
“Ed—I make 10 sales a day of a $149 product.”
So I said:
“Okay—let’s put it up to $249. And if you keep making the same sales—keep it.”
They go down? Drop it a bit. Until we find the price where the sales—you know—get steady.
And another client had been wanting to go from $500 to $700 but was waiting.
I said:
“Why would you wait? Just do it now. Let’s see what happens.
And if they keep buying it—we’ll know the value. And we can keep putting it up.”
And sure enough—sales came in.
So with this system, what we’re actually going to do is find your price.
So to start, you could offer a limited space early bird discount.
And then when that’s gone, you just look and see:
Does it keep selling at the non-discounted price?
If the answer is yes—we keep going. With the aim of getting up more and more and more over time.
And once people have done it and given you testimonials—it’s going to be easy to sell too.
But if sales are struggling—we keep it lower until we have more proof of results and have worked out how to optimize the marketing so that we can charge more.
Price is so weird.
I have a friend who took his offer from $150 to $1,500—and sold more when it was more expensive.
Because sometimes—people want the more expensive option more than they do the cheap one.
So remember that.
Promotion
So once that’s ready, the next step is a little more promotion. Because we need to then work out how to promote the value add and the discount and the launch.
And to get your message out, what we’re going to do is—we’re going to write and send seven emails.
And I know what you’re thinking—“Oh, you’re going to annoy people.”
Don’t panic. It’s fine.
In the history of doing this, nobody has ever died from sending or receiving a few emails.
Apart from Steve.
I miss you, Steve.
Yes, you’re going to get some unsubscribers—but if you have a waitlist, they will have actually said:
“I want to know when this thing’s coming out, so please let me know.”
So they won’t get annoyed.
But also—don’t freak out when people unsubscribe.
It just means your email gets cheaper. It’s actually a good thing.
So here’s the simple framework we use for this:
Email 1 to the waitlist: Just hype that the thing is coming very soon.
Email 2: Tease a little more, but tomorrow you’re going to open it.
Email 3: Announce the opening of the sales. Keep the email short and get into the sales page to learn more.
Email 4: Very educational. You give them a nugget of wisdom and then you give them a link to the product too.
Email 5: A story of success that shows people you’re credible—that also links to your product.
Email 6: Final short promo.
Email 7: Just a short reminder letting them know that the discount or the value add or whatever it is is about to expire.
If at any point you get close to selling out the discounted option or the value add—just stop it, and just make sure that you email everyone to let them know it’s running out.
Production
All right—so now you have that, you then are going to go on finally to producing your actual product.
And to do that, you just start with the result you promised, and work out:
What’s the minimum I can give them to get there?
Not the maximum.
We only want the things that matter to get the result—so that you end up with a quality garden shed, not a skyscraper falling to bits.
So to do that, just plan out the steps and any assets you might be able to create to help them get there—like a to-do list or a worksheet.
So if this is like a 7-day meal prep planning workshop—we know the goal is to help them prep all of that food in an hour.
So you just bullet point how you’re going to do that, and then build from there.
So you would make some PDFs that have recipes on, and then the rest might just be videos—and it’s kind of it.
So just remember: this workshop needs to solve a specific problem in the simplest way possible—and ideally it’s something they can do in like an hour or 90 minutes.
So they get a fast result, and they’ll value it a lot more then.
Which brings us on to step nine.
We just launch it and we wait and see.
Flops
But what happens if you launch and this doesn’t make you many sales—or any sales?
Well—this is what’s cool about marketing.
What you tend to find is—the lessons you learn from a flop are worth more than what you would have made from a hit—so long as you work out why it didn’t go to plan.
And there’s only really a few things that can go wrong.
So firstly—you want to look at how many people landed on your sales page, which most landing page builders will track.
If it’s like a thousand people and you made no sales—that means something about the offer or sales page put them off.
But it also means the marketing that drove them there was probably working quite well.
And what’s crazy is—sometimes sales page conversions could take a hit because you put the price at the very top.
So everyone looked at it and went, “$149? No thanks.”
Because they didn’t value it. They didn’t read anything. They didn’t watch the video.
So it could be something small.
The other thing that’s very likely if you have this sort of error is that you launched an offer your viewers don’t associate you with.
So if you have a channel that’s not devoted to this topic, and it just touches on it here and there in a few videos—that’s going to hurt.
This is going to stop it from making sales.
We need our channel and our offer to be very similar.
Then the next thing you want to look at is: if the amount of traffic that went to the sales page was low, you’ve got a bottleneck there.
So you ask—why?
And that could just be that your emails didn’t get many clicks. Or your emails didn’t get opened.
If the link clicks were low—you have an email problem.
If the opens were low—you have a list or a headline problem.
This could mean:
You’ve not emailed them enough
You’ve gone into spam
Your headlines weren’t appealing
So this is basically just a game of looking at just one of these few steps and going:
Where was the data not very good?
And then just fixing the bottleneck there—before you then drive more traffic at it from your videos.
So really—as long as you’ve made a great little product that you’re very proud of and you think is good—it’s not game over.
Because you can:
Promote this in every video you make
Mention it in your content
Share the wins of people who took it
Get more traffic to it
And then learn where to fix it over time
Based like that.
But what if your viewers get annoyed with you for selling?
Well—think of it like this.
Imagine if you met someone at a party, and you hit it off. And they said to you:
“Hey, really—can I come over for dinner?”
And you're like:
“Okay yeah, sure.”
And then they turned up and they put a giant bag of Herbal Life down on your dining room table and started to sell it to you.
That is the feeling viewers will have when you go from this friendly person who makes free content to selling in it.
But—if at that party you had met someone and they said:
“Oh yeah I got this job selling Herbal Life. It's going pretty amazing actually.”
And you’re like:
“Wow that sounds pretty cool.”
And they said:
“Well if you like, I could pop over tomorrow and show you how to sell it yourself.”
You'd say:
“Okay, come on over.”
So the expectations are not offensive when they then try to sell you their thing.
So letting people know that:
“Hey look—I’m a business, not a YouTuber.”
Does the same thing.
It’s why I always talk about my clients, my business, and what I offer in my videos in a way that’s not like:
“Buy my thing now!”
But it’s part of my story.
Because I’m a business with a YouTube channel—not a YouTuber trying to sell stuff.
And that stops you getting hate. And there’s a big difference.
Now admittedly, you do need to get as many return viewers and emails for this to really make you big money.
So if you are lacking there—what you want to do is watch this video next, and I’ll show you exactly how to create a million-dollar YouTube strategy that will drive people at it evergreen, in your sleep, forever—so you can make the big cash monies.
That was weird.

## Video 6 · Copy This Video, It’ll Blow Up Your Sales

Intro Summary
This video is one of the fastest and easiest I've ever made and one of the best performing for sales. And the framework I used to make it didn't just work once — it blew up my sales multiple times, used it in multiple videos, and got hundreds of emails too. So now I'm going to give you every step to make your own so that you can just copy it.
What is this video
So to start — what is this video and why should you make it? Well, anyone can copy a Google blog post and turn it into a video. But not everyone can point out a problem a viewer has, then show them how to fix it in their own unique way.
So the reason that these videos are so powerful is because you get your viewers to send in something for you to review, and then in the video, you review it and you tell them how to fix the problems they have — or how to level up to get a result.
And this proves you're legit. It sets you apart from others in the niche. And it will massively raise your credibility with undeniable proof of your genius.
And that is why these things will make you so much money.
So in this example, you can see I just gave feedback on my viewers’ channels or offers — and look: made insane money.
Can this video work for anyone
But can this style of video work for anyone?
Well, I mean, if you're a dating channel, you could review app profiles. If you're a fitness one, you could review squat form. If you're in the music niches, you can review viewers’ guitar playing or drumming, right?
So a lot of channels can harness this style. So don’t just write it off if it’s not obvious how you could use it too — because it could be a massive opportunity for your business.
But also, you need to try these, 'cause a while ago I wanted to post more often, but making videos takes up a huge amount of time, right? So I asked myself: what is the fastest and easiest way for me to do this? And I realized it was just giving feedback on people’s work — which is basically something I do day in, day out with my clients. So it comes very naturally.
So this style of video is potentially going to be much faster for you to make as a result too.
Now, the way I look at them is like this: I don’t expect these videos to get crazy views or blow up. Now, they can do very well, as you can see — but what I want them to do really is convert my viewers to buyers. And for that, they are exceptional.
