All right — so now you know why you need to do these, here's the system to make your own.
Step 1: Crack your title
What you need to do is crack your title. But that can take ages. So I've just come up with three that you can use:
The first one, which I really like, is:
“I studied [X things] — here’s why [bad thing] — and how to [good thing]”
For example:
“I studied a thousand basketball players — here’s why they can’t slam dunk.”
The next title you can try is simply:
“Roasting viewers’ [X]”
And another one you could try is:
“Rating my viewers’ [X]”
And these have done well for years over YouTube.
Now, I like the “I studied” title ‘cause it implies that you’ve put a lot of work into the video — which will raise your credibility.
But how do you get that number in the title nice and big, so the viewers go “Ooh that’s looking juicy”?
Step 2: Get signups
Well this is where step two comes in — where all you do is you make a Google Form and you post it to your community wall to get signups.
Now, the form itself needs to give people the ability to add their name and their email like this — and then you just ask them to submit the thing that you're going to review, as well as ask them the number one problem they currently have.
Then if you have an email list, write a post like this, just saying:
“Hey, I’m going to give you this opportunity to get [whatever it is] reviewed. Join my list and I’m going to send out the form later.”
This way you're going to grow your email list too. And this is how I can claim to have studied hundreds of people in my YouTube titles — 'cause I have over 1,700 people who have submitted to this form since I started doing them.
But even if you only have like 20 people — this can still work.
Now, if you don’t get many applications, just switch to one of the other titles that doesn’t require a nice big number to be interesting.
Step 3: Thumbnails
All right — so step three of this system we want to look at is how basic these thumbnails are.
On screen — look. It’s just me and some text. And the text creates some curiosity combined with the title.
But there is a trick to making these images work well with your content — so that they match your viewers’ expectations after the click (which is critical for keeping them engaged and getting more views too).
And it works like this:
When I first did this style of video, I noticed everyone who submitted their offers to be reviewed by me was selling courses — rather than selling the benefit a course brings in their marketing.
So I said in the thumbnail:
“Look, stop selling courses”
Which creates curiosity, as viewers go:
“Wait — hang on. Why shouldn’t I do that?”
So they click.
What you want to do is go through your submissions and look for the most common problem you see — and then in the thumbnail, say:
“Stop doing the thing that is causing it.”
And feature it in your video. Hopefully it’s something they’re all doing.
Step 4: Plan
So the next step’s going to stop this becoming a planning nightmare. It’s going to save you a ton of time, and it’s going to give you the secret to sales success.
To show you how this works: I have loaded all of the comments from the video that generated a ton of sales into my free AI bot (that you can get below) — and then the AI has broken it down, told me what exactly viewers loved about the video.
And as a result, I then designed a framework to plan your own around.
So what the AI bot pulled out here is that the viewers love:
Seeing a transformation from bad to good
Then understanding how to get it for themselves
Which is something regular videos often lack.
So in this example that you can see on screen right now, what I’m doing is I’m playing a viewer’s intro, then saying what’s wrong with it, then I rewrote it, read it out, and then gave some tips to help the viewers get better results on their own scripts too.
So the simple framework you run through this video is:
Pick a viewer with a problem
Show their problem
Say why it’s bad
Fix the problem or show another example that doesn’t have it
Explain why it works
But if you can’t visually show a transformation — don’t panic, because this can still work.
‘Cause after that, we also give viewers 1–3 action points so they can level up faster too.
So in a dream world we show a transformation — but if you can’t, just review the viewer submission and then give three steps to fix it.
And that’s what makes this really powerful.
You don’t have to try and solve every problem your viewers have.
You just need to give viewers a couple of things they could action right away — for this to level up your credibility and get your bank account pinging.
Step 5: Write your intro
Then once you’ve got your plan, you move on to your next step — which is writing your intro.
So I’m going to play the intro of the video that generated $71,000 — and then I’m going to break it down so you can copy what works.
“Are the first 15 seconds of your video stopping you from making money?
Recently, thousands of creators applied to be roasted by me on this channel, and most of them were making mistakes that will wreck trust instantly with their viewers.
And that will kill watch time, link clicks, and sales.
So today, I’m going to roast three creators and show you how to fix their intro problems — that you probably have too — using some tricks that have generated millions.”
Okay, so let’s look at the script on the screen here.
So the first thing I do is I ask a question that’s designed to raise some curiosity.
So you basically say:
“Is the thing you know is causing your viewers an issue, actually causing them an issue?”
For example:
Is your diet plan the real reason you can’t lose weight?
Is the way you’ve been taught to play scales on the guitar stopping you from leveling up faster?
Is your dating profile repelling your future partner?
Then here, what we do is we undo the clickbait nature of the title by saying you studied the amount of people you said you did — and that most of them are making the mistake that will wreck the sales they want.
And then you say you’re going to review three viewers to show them how to fix the problem — to get the result they want.
Then I always like to make sure my intros have some credibility in them — so I say:
“This generated me millions.”
So all you need to do is just give a little bit of proof that it’s going to work — if they stick around to the end.
Step 6: Get emails
Okay, so the next step is going to get you a lot of emails.
And you can see I’ve generated hundreds from this video doing what I’m about to show you.
All you need to do is — in the first 90 seconds — just tell the viewers:
“If you want to submit to your next review, they can sign up below.”
And then you just leave the sign-up link below the video and try to remind them to do this three times in the video for ultimate sign-ups.
But do not say “Check out the link below to sign up to my next roast.”
So the first time I promoted this, I put it on my community wall and a thousand people entered it.
But when I said “Submit to my next roast” — it got 20 signups.
So call it a “free review.”
Because when you say “I’m going to roast you,” it sounds like you’re going to like… tear them apart.
And a lot of people don’t want to be torn apart, right? It’s like — maybe not a positive experience for them.
Step 7: Record
All right — so let’s move on to the next step, where you just shoot the scripted intro.
But you don’t have to write a script for the rest of the video if you don’t want to.
Instead, you just use that bullet point structure I gave you — which is all planned out — and then you simply record your screen and then use it as a guide.
So what I do is I use QuickTime to record my screen, and then I have that camera — this one, hello — rolling at the same time.
And that’s it.
Step 8: Editing
Which brings us on to the next step of the system — which I think I’d do a bit differently now if I was starting over with the format.
So my first attempt at filming this style of video gave me about 45 minutes of footage — and because I had no script and the process wasn’t very well optimized, I rambled a lot.
And I kind of feel like the video is way too long.
So in the edit I cut out a ton — which took flipping ages.
Looking back, I think that was a mistake.
What I would do now if it was my first attempt is to just not worry too much about cutting out loads of content or trying to make the perfect video — and I’d just get the video out with minimal editing instead, just to see what happened.
Now, if you made a mistake, or you nipped to the loo halfway through and there’s 10 minutes of dead air — cut out all of that.
But otherwise, just keep this really simple to start.
Because I think if we do the minimum viable option first — and it works well for views and emails and sales — we maybe don’t need to do much more.
And if this minimum version does work, we’ve got a really fast format to remake in the future.
But if we’re not happy with the results and we think maybe it could be better — well, we know we can improve it next time, right?
So then we can bolt on more work and more complexity if needs be.
Step 9: Tracking
Okay, so after that there’s another step that we can’t ignore.
So look at this roast.
It generated a lot of sales — but didn’t get many views.
So if I’d just gone by the YouTube Studio’s views data, I’d have probably not made another one of these — because, well, if we’re just basing results on views, it’s average, right?
So why repeat something average?
But with RevTracks data, I could see YouTube was wrong — and that it was actually amazing for my business.
So I kept doing them.
So when you upload a video, you have to track its impact on your business — so you can instantly see if it’s getting sales and emails.
Otherwise, how on earth do you know what to repeat?
And if this style of video is even worth your time and effort?
Because the whole idea of this style of video really isn’t about views — it’s what I call conversion content.
So when you see this sort of thing making you money, you don’t really care if the views are lower.

The thing is, I put it out and got about 40 people applying, but then I realized I’d shot myself in the foot here. The application was kind of long, and meant vetting it would take ages. So I thought, hang on a sec—let me just see if AI can pick the best candidates for the role.
So I let it know what mattered most to me, and then I fed in the answers from the forms. And I asked it for the top five candidates—and it told me to hire someone called Rouser.
Now, at this stage I had a bit of a moral dilemma, because these people had put quite a lot of time into filling out these forms, and they'd done all the tasks I'd asked. So I thought, look, I can't do this. I've got to give them the time back and check out their work.
So I did. I spent the afternoon manually going through them, and I came up with three candidates I thought were the best. I emailed them, got it down to two, I had Zooms with some of them—and guess who I hired? Rouser.
It was absolutely spot on.
So this isn't just about hiring people. This AI is about asking yourself: what am I about to do that I don't want to? And how can I get AI to do it for me?
AI 4 - Get more emails
This brings me on to the next way to use AI, which generated me thousands of emails in a few minutes' work.
So one of the most critical things you have to do if you want to keep viewers engaged is find ways to show them the problem they have—and then show them how to solve it. If you can do this, you give them light bulb moments, and you make information feel new, which converts to sales and gets your videos more traction.
1
So I wanted to make a video to show viewers why they needed to improve conversion rates on their landing pages—not just get more views. But I was struggling, because in order to show them the problem, I wanted to build a calculator. They’d then put their monthly views into it and it would say, "This is how much you should be making." But I didn’t know how to make that.
So I got ChatGPT01 model and said, “This is what I want to do—how could I build it? I can't code.” And it tells me to do it in a spreadsheet and then spits out the instructions on how to build it.
But I looked at it and I thought, I don't know how to do this either. So I just fed it to ChatGPT Operator and it built the whole thing for me. And I used it in the video. And then I told the viewers they could download it if they wanted to try it too—and ended up with thousands of emails.
Now, it didn’t do a perfect job. I had to fix one thing with the spreadsheet. But my gosh, are we on the verge of getting rid of tasks like this forever.
AI 5 - Grow your business
Which brings me on to another way to use AI to earn more and work less.
So I have a problem. I can't just pump loads of people into my program, because we provide a lot of support, and I don’t want to overload it. Now, one solution to fix this is always just to build a bigger team, right? But I'm not a manager. I'm just a guy that likes making stuff.
So having a big team doesn't excite me. And this is why I have no full-time staff at all. And I know this is not very business-like—especially from someone teaching people to grow businesses. But I'm in the game for the love of it. So I only do the things I love.
So the fun challenge I have is: how can I grow a business and provide the best service I can and continue to improve things to make my clients' lives easier and easier—without needing more and more people?
Now obviously I'm still hiring people like Rouser. But I have doubled my turnover and have support inquiries from clients—and it's not because I'm ignoring them.
So if you have clients, or a program, or a course, it's likely what you're going to find is 80% of the questions you get asked you've answered a million times.
So if you want to scale without bloating, do this: just write down the biggest problems your clients have and the most common questions they ask.
But that's step one. We then—if we can—go a bit deeper. 
So if you're like me, you might have a chat room with tens of thousands of messages from clients in that you've built up over time.
What I did was I downloaded the entire chat history and I used AI to analyze this data to try and discover what questions were asked the most, the top problems people had, the biggest areas of confusion, which chat room had the most time spent in it by them and the team and myself.
And getting this data then revealed the number one issue that people struggled to work out was their niche—then how to position their channel to bring viewers back and generate sales, and then writing.
So I spent a few days building an AI bot whose sole purpose was to work out their niche for them and tell them how to position their channel around it.
This AI bot has been a complete game changer. I have had comments from my clients telling me it's the best thing I've ever done—which is a little offensive, 'cause there's some pretty cool stuff. But the result is that number one issue that everyone used to struggle with is now not even in our top five asked questions.
I'm not saying I've totally eradicated it—it's still a complex thing. But it's a heck of a lot better.
But not only that—six months ago I was spending about an hour a day giving feedback in the client intro feedback room. But now, thanks to AI, it's about 10 minutes.
Because all clients do is use my AI feedback coach, and then they submit that output to us. And instead of having to explain to them everything that's not working in their original script, they're writing these absolutely amazing pieces where we just look at it and go:
"Maybe delete that line… and yeah you're good. That's it."
So because of the success of this, we have an AI for email feedback, one for general FAQs—which I hate to admit, but it's actually better at answering most of the questions it gets asked than me because it doesn't get tired, and it's not dyslexic, so it can actually spell.
Now before you think "Oh Ed's gone all AI," 'cause people think I'm wearing these glasses 'cause I'm an AI avatar—no.
Our product, our support—nothing is all AI-based. The human element is still critical. Because AI is not going to spot everything, whereas the pros will.
So this is about using your expertise to get your clients faster support—which they love—and then checking that support and then leveling it up if it needs it.
You need to ask yourself this now
And that brings us on to the AI I showed you at the very start. What is it? Where did it come from?
Well, after about a year of just non-stop playing with AI, I've proven that it can get my clients leveled up faster, get them unstuck, take them from E-level to B+ level on intros—and now I'm turning all of the bots I showed you into one that we custom build around their own unique situation, so that we put those guardrails up on their channel to stop them making the common mistakes that put their buyers off and their viewers off.
So what I showed you at the start is a custom AI I've built for my program. But the reason I want you to see this isn't because I'm trying to make it some showy ad—although I do like to show off a little bit.
It's because there's a really serious question you have to ask yourself right now. And I honestly think this is the difference between making millions and going to zero over the next 2 years.
So when I was a kid, a parent complained when my school bought a computer. I remember them arguing—they said, "You should have bought library books instead. This thing's a waste of money."
And then when the internet came out, they said it would never catch on and that nobody would ever make card payments online—'cause it was clearly a scam.
And I remember sitting with Dad and he bought something with a card and he instantly called his bank to make sure it was okay—'cause we were both like "What is this thing?"
Then social media blew up and people laughed at me for vlogging a trip to Japan and putting it online. They said "What are you doing, you weirdo—just walking around talking to a camera."
And then when Bitcoin appeared everyone said "Oh this is a dumb idea—it's going to zero."
Look at the patterns that have repeated themselves over the last 20 years.
We have had multiple trillion-dollar opportunities. And all of the people who got in early—who are often not crazy smart people, just kind of curious—made millions whilst everyone else told them it was a scam and a fad and would never catch on, and it wasn’t actually good enough to put the time and effort into yet.
So right now, if you're not trying to implement AI into your business and your channel, you're just putting yourself in the "I'm going to get in too late and then regret it later" club.
AI is just not too early to be useful. Right now it's so good—and it's only getting better.
You just need to be using it now so that when it hits the next level, you already know how to use it. And then when it levels up again, all of the systems you built with it suddenly get even better—without you having to do anything.
So where do you start if you don't want to join the billions of people who are going to sit there going "I wish I got in on AI"?
Well, just write down the top three problems your clients have and ask AI—one by one—to help you come up with ways to solve it. So I use ChatGPT01 model for strategic thinking and 4.5 for creative thinking.
And then just write down the top three problems you have in your own business and do the same.
And then just get used to chatting to it like it's a coach that you would pay a lot of money to talk to.
I talk to AI 3 to 6 hours a day now. I don't even type to it—I voice note it on my app or I get my Mac to transcribe everything I'm saying. So this is just getting insanely fast.
But it's critical to note that we need to continue the human element in our services. The future I see is AI doing the brunt of the work, the humans kind of supporting it, and then adding their take on it.
And if you're like "Oh, I think I need to take this more seriously now," then watch this video next—it's going to give you some more AI tricks you can use right now. And they're a great place for a beginner.
Please don't sleep on it.
You've been warned.
—Rob

Why do people get addicted to things?

That's the same feeling your viewers get when your video doesn't match what they thought they were clicking on.
1
And when that happens, they don't watch your video.
And then they don't watch even more. And they don't come back.
And if that happens, you won't make any sales.
But when your video does match their expectations, they think "Oh yes. This is exactly what I love."
1

Meaning your potential clients—who might be, say, age 40+—are not going to click the same kind of videos that a 20-year-old or an 18-year-old or a 15-year-old would watch.
And most of the YouTube education out there is telling you to make what works for those youngsters who binge on entertainment—not businessy kind of content.
Right now, what that means for you is you don't just do exactly what you've been told to do and copy what works from other people's channels and bring that over to your own.

A 50-year-old who we're after ain't clicking on that.
Looks like it's packed full of E numbers.

He copied what worked for his people—and then he just did more and more of it.
So in step one, all you do is you match the style of your thumbnail to the expectations and desires of your actual buyers.
And that can mean no glowing effects, no neon text, no funky backgrounds—just simple, clear visuals that feel familiar and trustworthy, especially if your audience is a bit older.
And you'll know when you've nailed this because you're going to get more views, and your Audience tab is going to show you the right age group watching your content.
This tab on YouTube is one of the most important sections in the Studio as far as I'm concerned for a business.
Step Two — this is absolutely critical for getting people to come back (and that's how you generate sales), and it works like this:
So let's say you've nailed Step One. You made a really great-looking thumbnail our audience is going to love. It's high quality. It's clean. It's classy.
And then our viewer comes along, and they click play… and they get this.
This is an instant disconnect. Viewers clicked on a Mars bar—and they got given a rice cake.
And when that happens, your viewer’s trust is instantly gone. And it's very hard to get back.
So what should you do instead?
Well, you've probably seen creators with really sketchy-looking thumbnails and like zero production quality.
And you've wondered—how on earth are they getting so many views?
It doesn't make sense when I'm putting in so much work.
Well, one big reason is because their viewers see that scraggly-looking thumbnail—and then they hit play—and the content and the scraggliness of the thumbnail is in line.
The packaging matches the product.
So they stay.
So what should you do? Go high-end? Or keep your production quality scrappy-looking?
Well, again—it kind of depends on who you're trying to attract.
So what you want to do is you want to ask yourself this:
What kind of production quality would appeal to the people I want to work with, or to sell my products to?
So for our retirement channel example:

But always try to make sure you're matching your setup to that style that builds trust with your buyer and is the most in line with your thumbnail and title.
Which moves us on to Step Three—and this one has to work in harmony with your thumbnail, your setup, your format—if you want to meet your viewers’ expectations and keep them coming back to the chocolate roll.

Well, again—it all comes down to audience expectations.

But the takeaway from this is:
Your presentation style has to match the type of video you're making—and the expectations you’ve set with your thumbnail, title, and format.
So for our demo retirement channel—we've got to think:

Someone who's calm and collected?
Someone who radiates confidence?
Or some guy cracking jokes, using props, and dancing?
And there’s not necessarily a right and wrong answer.
And I think if you comment below which one you'd rather see—we’ll probably see that.
Okay, so this next part of the process is going to save you from one of the biggest YouTube mistakes out there:
Chasing a flat perfect retention line.
Yeah. The thing everyone tells you to get and obsess over.
I used to chase that too.
But let's go back a couple of years.
So back then, my videos were all about inspiration, motivating the viewers so they got to the end and they felt good.
And I hoped that that would make them want to watch another one—'cause it was like educational crack.
But to do that you had to make the info really kind of light.
And I'd throw in lots of cinematic B-roll and emotional music and fancy transitions and effects.
And the retention graph looked like this.
And every now and then, I would get retention so flat it would look like someone drew it with a ruler in sections.
But now my videos do this.
And that has played a critical part in my business’s profit sorium—because I'm not trying to keep everyone happy.
Now I’m intentionally making videos that turn the wrong viewers away.
It’s why at the start of every video I say “businesses” or “sales” and “products”—because the majority of people in the YouTube space who should still be tempted to click on a video that is YouTube-businessy—it will put them off.
Because they hate those words and they don't want anything to do with it.
But my ideal clients—they go “That's exactly what I want. It sounds great.” So they stay.
So now I produce the total opposite of what I used to.
And I’ve banned music. I’ve banned sound effects. Zooms. Cinematic B-roll.
I literally pay Greg—who edits for me—not to edit for me as much.
Sorry Greg. I know it's boring. But it's better for mankind we do it this way.
So if you’re panicking because your intro’s dropped hard, just ask this instead:
Did I get more comments?
Did I get more shares?
Did I get more email signups or product sales?
And if the answer is yes—then what are you worrying about?
The video worked.
That graph can really trap you into making mass-market content.
Really, a healthy decline is not that big a deal.
It’s actually a good thing sometimes.
Now, don’t get me wrong—I still check my retention graph because it can give me some other clues.
And I still do want to see flat bits here and there, and I still want to learn from it.
But if you focus on it too much, it tricks you into making content for the masses instead of making content for your one dream client.
And if you’re building a business, that’s the difference between struggling and scaling.
So for our retirement channel—I’m saying:
No stock footage. No music. No flashy cuts. Boring-ass looking videos.
I’m going to use some text on screen.
Basically what you’re seeing right now.
Which brings us onto our next step—Which, if you want people to keep coming back—and you want those viewers turning into leads and sales—this part is a non-negotiable.
So let me show you how I'm doing it on this very channel that you're watching right now.
So when I pivoted from my old channel, Film Booth (which you can see here), I wasn’t just tweaking my strategy — I was starting from scratch.
Because I was after a new audience.
I'm after a new age group, and they have new problems.
And if I want to create that chocolate drawer effect, I need to go on a discovery mission to figure out — well — what does this audience actually want?
So for six weeks, I doubled up my output (as you can see here), and I started testing formats like a man possessed.
And when I say formats, I mean the style of the video:

But after that — I didn’t need to guess what to make any more.
Because I was looking at the data.
I was reading the comments.
I was trying to work out:
What do you guys love about those things I’ve produced?
What brought in the leads?
What got return viewers?
What actually built the business?
Then, when I had that data — I just did more of what worked.
Now I use tracking software to see which formats bring in the most sales.
It’s pretty much impossible to play this YouTube game without it.
Join the income tracking train in the description. Choo choo.
Oh no, that’s bad. Still going to workshop that slogan.
But here’s the thing:
If you find one thing that works, you can’t just repeat it over and over again forever.
Because eventually, what happens is — your viewers will feel like they’re eating the same chocolate over and over again.
And what happens when you do that?
Well, they eventually get bored of it.
And you’ll see the impact of this on channels who blew up with one particular format…
and then just used it over and over again.
Eventually, their views get less… and less… and less.
So here’s how you avoid that:
Your goal should be to find three to four formats that work — and then you rotate them on the channel.
These become your go-to content types that you know your viewers love, that always bring people back — and you just keep doing the same thing over and over again.
But in between those staples — you experiment.
So:
You try something weird.
You try something new.
You try something a bit quirkier.
You don’t have to do that.
The idea is — you’re looking for one to hit — and then boom — you’ve got a new repeatable format.
Or something better to replace one that’s going stale.
This is how you evolve a channel while staying fresh and familiar at the same time…
and build long-term viewership that actually grows a business.

and I work with hundreds of businesses, and we all share stuff in my community.

## Video 9 · Copy This YouTube Strategy, It'll Blow Up Your Business

Most businesses waste their time and miss out on a ton of consistent sales from YouTube because they overlook the six critical steps for generating leads and income that should be baked into every single video that you make.
So in this video I'm going to show you the exact sales system I use to drive thousands of leads, calls and make $1.2 million in the first 3 months of 2025 alone without any ads. And oh yeah — it's going to get you more views too.
Okay so to start, you need to understand the strategy we're going to use to generate sales and why it is so powerful. So let me show you behind the scenes of my channel now so that you can see how this works.
All right — so check out this views per hour metric down the side here. You'll have it too. And then notice how three or four videos are pulling in 70 to 100 views an hour all the time on the channel.
That's what we want to see. But to some of you it's going to feel like a lot. To others, not that many. But the reason having multiple videos pushed at the same time like this is a complete gamecher for a business — even if it's 10 views an hour or 10,000 views an hour per video — is because all YouTube wants to do is keep people on the platform watching.
So when someone discovers one of your videos, YouTube is keeping track of what else you made that they clearly enjoyed. So if it works out that four of your videos have created high viewer satisfaction with viewers, it will try to get more people to watch those four.
So you can see on this graphic here — this is what's happening on my channel. A new viewer discovers a video — so let's say it's this video. And then YouTube recommends they watch one of these four others because its data tells them people like them. So it keeps pushing them.
The thing is, once someone's watched one of the four videos YouTube keeps pushing, it'll keep showing them the other three they've not seen too. So these four videos are basically referring each other all of the time.
And that's why they're generating 70 to 100 views an hour — because they work as a team like this.
But that's not the best thing about this for businesses — because imagine if each of these videos was optimized to generate sales and leads as well. So they didn't just get views — they grew your business harnessing this system 24/7 on autopilot.
That's exactly what I'm going to show you how to build right now with a very simple six-step strategy — which is harder to say than it is to do.
Step 1
All right, so step one — this is really important because if this fails, you can't really start the system in the first place. And all you need to do is three very specific things in your intro. So let me show you how this works.
So here's my script and I'm going to read it out and then I'm going to pull out what those three things are:
"Most businesses waste time and miss out on more consistent sales from YouTube because they overlook the six critical steps for generating leads and income that should be baked into every video you make. So in this video I'm going to show you the exact system I use to drive thousands of leads, calls, and make $1.2 million in the first three months of 2012 alone without any ads. And oh yeah — it can also get you more views too."
So the first thing you have to check off is why someone should care about the coming content, which I did right here in this first line where I set up the problem.
And then in the second line I did it too — where I said what they would learn if they stuck around. That’s it.
Now the next thing every intro needs is just to show that you are credible and worth listening to, which I did here in the second line where I talk about some of my accolades.
And then the third step is just to try and keep this thing under 15 seconds. That’s it.
If you can tick off all those three things, the referral network's going to work really well for you.
Step 2
Which then moves us on to step two — which is going to rehook viewers and it's going to get them past the first third of a video.
So when people get past the first third of a video, they're just more likely to stay to the very end. And the end is where the sales and leads magic happens. So we really need to get them there.
So for this step what you want to do is you want to ask yourself what's my most killer insight that I want to talk about in this video — and then frontload it right after your intro.
So a killer insight is simply putting what you think your best point might be for your target audience early. I mean it makes sense, right? If the viewers are instantly blown away, they’re going to listen to everything else that follows — even if those points are maybe not quite as strong.
Step 3
Which moves us on to step three — which is the bridge from views to leads and sales. And it works like this.
So have a look at these two videos. The first one here has significantly more views, yet fewer link clicks and leads. The second one has less views overall — yet it brings in way more clicks and leads.
Why is this happening?
Well, it's because I use step three of the system — and I just ask viewers to click the link to sign up to my email in the first 90 seconds of a video. That is it.
Asking sooner just makes all the difference. So imagine if every video in your referral network did this. If you're getting hundreds of views an hour, you're going to be funneling a huge amount of people into your email — which is a complete gamecher.
Step 4
And that brings us on to step four of this system — which is the solution to a massive problem all of us face.
So YouTube and the internet have been around forever now. And because of that, the chances are your viewers have already heard the same tips a thousand times over. So if you give them things they already know, it kind of wrecks your content — which wrecks the referral network system because they get bored and they leave.
But how can you possibly know what viewers already know to get around this? Well — you can't.
But that doesn't matter if you do what I did in this example that you can see on screen right now.
So you've probably watched videos where someone says, "You need to increase your sales page conversion." And it's like "Yeah duh. Of course that makes sense." Like it's not that useful.
The thing is, I wanted to make that point in a video. So in this example, instead of just repeating the obvious that everyone's heard a million times before — what I did was create a simple spreadsheet where I loaded in the amount of views a channel had, and then showed the viewers how many sales they could expect based on the different conversion rates of the sales page.
What that meant was viewers could now see the problem for themselves in a unique way that made the information feel new.
I also told viewers that they could access the sheet for themselves using the link below the video — which doubled up as a lead magnet and generated thousands of emails as a result.
So the secret to driving people from one video to the next and creating the referral network is making your information feel new. So even if people have heard it before, they remained hooked.
But do you have to build a spreadsheet like I did? Well, no.
Making information feel new can actually be a lot simpler. So for example, in this other video here I made a triangle to help people understand the different types of YouTube creators they could be. So instead of saying "You need to present like Mr Beast," I showed them a triangle and said each corner was a different creator style — and you just need to lean into one of these corners more than another.
Step 5
Now the next step is deceptively simple — but incredibly powerful.
In fact, this tiny little tweak is the main reason why this video here got way more leads than this other one.
What did I do differently?
Well, by tracking my videos’ links and clicks and signups I realized that all you need to do is at the end of your video, simply remind viewers to take the action you asked them to take in the first 90 seconds again.
So if you're promoting an email or a lead magnet, you say, "Don't forget to download my free planner before the video finishes."
And if you really want to turbocharge signups in your videos — here's what else tracking my videos has taught me.
You want to do three things:
You want to ask people to take an action in the first 90 seconds,
in the middle of the video,
and at the end.
That's it. And you don't even have to interrupt your video.
I mean look at this example on screen right now — I can just use some text on screen as that call to action.
Now imagine if all of your videos in your network do this. Your leads just explode.
Step 6
Which brings us on to the next step. And it's the glue that holds the referral network together so you can get consistent views, leads, sales — and look what it's done for my own. It’s crazy, right?
Okay — so it works like this.
So imagine you made a bunch of videos and you realize that one of them is amazing for generating emails and conversions. When you know this, all you do at the end of your video is tell viewers to watch it next by clicking your end screen.
And if you have four videos in your evergreen referral network all getting 100 views an hour — all pointing to this high converting video — you create this automated sales system that blows up your email list, calls, leads, and sales.
So that’s why tracking matters. Because if you don’t know what your videos are doing, how can you know which video to point people back to that’s going to grow your business?
If you include these six steps in every video you make, I can pretty much guarantee you're going to grow your business.
But now you know them, you need to make sure that you can turn those viewers into buyers once they’ve left YouTube too — by creating a system that sells in the way that people want to be sold to.
So watch this video next and I'll show you exactly how to turn viewers into buyers step by step.

## Video 11 · EVERYTHING You Need To Make Sales On YouTube

Have you ever wondered if your YouTube channel's actually ready to make money?
Well over the last 18 months I've been helping people launch businesses from their channels and many have flown, but some didn't do as well.
But what that meant was I got a way better idea of what the signs are that you are ready to make big money from your channel. So in this video I'm going to show you exactly what they are and how to check them for yourself.
But before we get on to that, you first need to work out if your niche can actually make you money in the first place.
So to help you establish how profitable your niche is, I want you to check out this triangle.
All right, so the width of this triangle represents your audience size, and then down the bottom we have the general audience, which is like massive, and then at the top we have the pointy bit—and that represents more niche channels with like a much smaller audience.
The thing is, as you go up this triangle the audience size becomes more valuable, which means you can charge a lot more to your product or service to a smaller group.
And this is how people make money from YouTube with less views.
The thing is, as you can see there are three different tiers here that make up this chart. So what you need to do is ask yourself: which one do you fall into?
So let me give you an example of each of these tiers with a demo channel that's all about golf.
Okay, so believe it or not, the biggest audience a golf channel could attract is absolutely everyone.
So if their strategy was just to try and attract as many people as possible, they would make a video called Why the World's Best Golfers Always Wear Red.
Now that video could appeal to so many people because it's kind of weird, right? And you're probably thinking to yourself, I wonder why someone would wear red. What would that do? Should I wear red?
Even though you maybe don't care about golf at all.
Now, a channel built on this type of mass market interest content can't really sell anything that expensive to the viewers, though, because those viewers didn't come to them with a problem.
They just came because they found it interesting. And solving a problem is how you earn money.
So let's move on to the next tier up.
Now, in this world, the creator makes a video called Five Ways to Drive As Far As Rory Mroy and their audience could be people who just love playing golf.
Now because people are obsessed with playing golf, we spend a lot of time and money on the hobby. So this tier I call the hobbyist level, and it's for channels who teach hobbyists a skill.
It could be painting, music, DJing, golf, kung fu—the list is massive.
Now, traditionally people said these niches were not worth that much money and all they could really sell is like cheap courses and PDFs.
And I kind of used to agree... until I started working with more people in it and witnessed them charging from $6,000 to $30,000 to hobbyists for an offer.
So this level is still an amazing way to make a living via YouTube.
And then we have up here the top of the triangle.
And our fake creator in this world would make something called Three Ways to Train Your Caddy Like Rory Maroy.
It's a really hard thing to say, can't imagine Rory trains his caddy or if that's even how it works—but just shut up, golf nerd. Stop bashing me in the comments, it doesn't matter. Let me make my point.
So this channel is in the top tier because they have a much smaller audience, but think of the impact a video like that could have on someone's life.
It could make them win a tournament, and that would then bring them a ton of money, right?
So the top of this triangle is usually referred to as the health, wealth, and relationship sector.
These are basically channels who deliver info that makes people money, makes them get over pain or live longer or gets them fitter or helps them get like a husband or a wife or works on their mindset.
These are kind of like the biggest pain points and problems out there, where you don't need massive views to earn a living—because the bigger the problem, the more people will spend to get rid of it.
So all you do now is look at this and decide: which one are you in?
Now don't panic if you're not in the top.
I actually think the gap right now is in the hobbyist section because of all the things I'm seeing.
Don't go telling yourself you can't make this work because you teach people knitting.
Trust me. You can.
Okay, so now you know how valuable your niche might be, let's have a look at how to spot some signs that you have a very hot audience who are ready to buy something from you when you decide to promote it.
All right, so the first sign—it's kind of weird—but to me it's one of the most clear-cut signs, and it works like this:
So when I used to post videos to my old channel Film Booth, it always used to crack me up because I'd put a video out and then 10 seconds later I'd get four comments saying “First!”
And then sometimes those people would argue about who was actually first in the comments.
And eventually I realized this was actually playing quite a big part in my business growing.
Because when you get comments like that, it means firstly: people are really excited for your video.
If they think they can raise their status simply by appearing below your content, it gives you an idea of how much they respect you.
And respect—well, that's a big part of sales, right?
Now don't worry if this never happens to you. It's not going to happen to a lot of people—especially if your target audience is all over like 60.
They're not going to waste their time doing that.
But the next thing to look at here really is the comments you get in the first 48 hours of your video's life.
Really what you want to be seeing is like a 10%‑ish comment‑to‑view ratio.
The reason for this is because YouTube will generally push your videos to people who have watched you before.
So getting a lot of comments is kind of a sign that you have a hot audience of people who keep watching you.
And that's what we need to kind of grow a business.
However, if you got like 100 views and 2 comments—yes, it's a 2% ratio—but you do need thousands of views to get a good idea of this.
The second sign is this.
So when I was blowing up on Film Booth years ago, I used to post a video that was about 10 minutes long and after 60 seconds I'd have the same amount of views to likes.
Which always confused me 'cause I was like—how can you like it? You've not finished it yet!
But it became obvious over time that when you have a hot audience, they don't really care what comes out of your mouth.
They just want to support you and say thank you for your help over the years.
And they do that with an instant like.
So if in the first 24 hours you can get around a 10% like‑to‑view ratio, that's another sign you have a hot fan base.
The next is what I personally think is the most important metric on YouTube.
You're most likely going to see this stat associated with all your best-pulling videos.
Nobody ever really talks about it.
And the best example of this would be this video on screen right now.
So I posted it and all of my usual metrics that I track on upload didn’t make much sense.
And I was like, Oh... why is this one doing so well? In the first 24 hours in particular?
So I went here. And I went in here.
And I looked at how many shares it had.
And yeah, it was blowing everything else out the window... wind window... whoa wool—it was blowing everything else away.
Now, the book Contagious, if you've never read it, explains why sharing matters so much.
And they tell this story about an experiment that established people would rather share something they love with a friend than be paid money not to.
The reason this happens is because people want to take the credit for the result of what they've shared.
Basically, if they share something epic, it makes them appear smart and interesting or more in-the-know, which raises their status and enhances their reputation.
And they called this social currency.
So when your videos are so good people are like I have to share this with others, they become this social currency.
And these sharing signals—well, they grow your channel.
But also, they're a sign that your content's so good people are probably going to want to buy something from you.
Now our next sign is really simple.
I've always used a rough benchmark of around 3,000 views per video being a sign that you're kind of ready to make money from your audience—because it means you’ve got a decent-size return viewership there.
But before you go, "Oh goody!", you need to listen to the rest of these things, 'cause it's not quite as simple as that.
I've learned a lot about launching offers recently, and we need to go a few levels deeper now just to avoid any surprises.
Which brings us on to our next sign—that’s a continuation of the last—and it works like this:
So if you're in the top two tiers of our triangle, you need to think of your audience size as a bucket with a hole in it, that's always being topped up slightly by a tap.
Because what happens is a large percentage of your audience will be beginners.
And they go, "I'm going to take up the guitar!"
And then they get really into it, binge a channel, and then 3 weeks later they quit the guitar.
And they don't really care about it again.
And they don’t unsubscribe.
And basically what they do is—they leak out the bottom of your bucket.
So for channels like us, we’re always fighting that natural audience drop-off of quitters.
So to keep an eye on the hole in your bucket, you measure your audience data by going here to the Audience tab and looking at the 30- to 90-day return viewership.
But what’s a good amount?
Well actually, just keeping this monthly return viewer number the same every month could actually be a really good sign for a business.
But why?
Well, it's because it means you're replacing the people who naturally quit caring about your niche.
So it could mean that you're bringing enough people in who are new to replace those quitters.
So you're not standing still—you are kind of growing with new people, or you're meeting new people still.
Now if this number goes up and up and up, that's great.
But the goal here for me was always to have that around 10,000 people, because that's a lot of people—if they’re the right people—returning to your content.
But recently, after changing my content strategy, that number flew up to like 50,000+.
So just by posting more, you can bring people in.
Anyway—if this number is small or it’s going backwards, that’s probably meaning you don’t have a hot enough audience.
But maintaining it and growing it is a good sign.
Okay, so next we have the boring sign absolutely nobody wants to hear.
And I only really recently discovered just how important this is—when a couple of my clients’ launches didn’t work out as planned.
But after a couple of days’ distance from it, I kind of realized why.
So I’m pretty big on saying to them: Do not launch a product until you hit these metrics. And I say that a lot.
The thing is—these guys, they smashed through all of those metrics.
So I was smug. I was like, "This is going to be easy."
But after a few sleepless nights, it became clear what had happened to them.
So to make sales, you need a hot audience.
And they’d grown so fast, which blew up their email list—the people on it were not that hot.
They’d not really sent them that many emails.
So they kind of had a big cold audience, because they were sort of new.
So this next sign is one nobody likes, 'cause it’s simply: spending more time in the game.
Which makes sense, right?
I mean—who do you trust?
The person you've seen on YouTube once, or the one that you’ve been tuning into for years?
The thing is—how much time in the game do you need to avoid a situation like this for yourself?
Well… not that easy to answer, I'm afraid.
And I’m kind of working on figuring this out right now.
But I'd probably say: the six months after blowing up in your niche, and continuing to deliver lots of value, and bring lots of people back, and get people into your email, and emailing them, and building up a lot of trust—
That's going to be a real safe zone for you.
So I’d maybe think about that.
It’s never worth rushing this stuff.
Which brings me on to the next metric, which is this one:
So you see that big old spike?
That’s link clicks.
And the more links people click from below your videos, the more of a sign it is that they’re eager to find something else from you.
That can be link clicks to your website, it could be link clicks to your email.
The next sign is a continuation of this, but it will play a really critical part in your sales.
And it’s simply seeing that people are not just clicking your email—but they’re signing up and joining it too.
Now, if you’re not seeing a steady stream of new email subs, it could potentially mean that they’re not just that interested in hearing from you—
And that's a sign of a cold audience that won’t be up for buying anything that you sell.
Now the next is a metric we can track off of YouTube, and it’s basically going to tell you how engaged your hottest viewers are.
And believe it or not—your hottest fans are not on YouTube.
They’re the ones who sign up to your email because they want you to send them more stuff when you’re not making videos. “Give me more!”
So making sure your open rates are always around 46% to 50% each email is a good thing to do.
If you get that—it means they like hearing from you.
If you don’t—before you panic, just delete everyone who’s not opened for six months, and it’ll probably naturally shoot up.
I cull my list quite a lot. It keeps it in the sweet spot.
Now the next sign is like someone nailed a flipping neon one to your head, and it’s just seeing comments or getting emails from your viewers going: “How do I work with you? Help me.”
Now, if you have nothing to sell and people are trying to buy your time—
That really is the ultimate sign.
Now the next is this:
I know I’ve written a powerful email to my list when I wake up and there’s 80 replies in my inbox.
Replies are a sign you struck a chord with people—because people don’t tend to waste their time replying to emails unless they really valued it.
And getting replies is also a sign that your content is building up the trust and is getting really good—which is the name of the game for generating easier sales.
After that, this next sign is more relevant for those of you who already have an offer—and that is:
Let’s say you have a low-ticket thing or one-to-one calls, and they’re just selling out fast, and you have a waitlist, and everything’s going well...
That’s generally just a sign that you can sell something bigger.
But what if you’re not seeing these signs—or many of them?
These things happen when your content is so good, people go: “How do I work with that person?”
So if you’re rushing videos out,
if you’re regurgitating a blog post,
if you’re not great on camera,
if your strategy is not great,
if you don’t really understand your audience,
and you haven’t got to grips with storytelling—
Then you’re probably not going to be in a position where you should expect to see that yet.
However—regular practice will get you there, as well as time in the game.
Now if you want to learn how to make videos that do everything I’ve just said,
watch this video next—I break it down step by step for you.

And this went on for years. I always had this overriding stress of how am I going to get paid this month? Now, at that time, I just started watching YouTube videos to try and learn how to grow my business, how to do SEO, how to do ads. They’re so useful. I bet they never have to chase people for sales.”
And then I looked into it and they’re making so much money. And I found this whole niche and world of people making money online. So, I decided, right, you need to do what they’re doing in some way, shape, or form and make YouTube videos.
So, I got out a spreadsheet and calculated: if I could grow a channel 5% a month for 10 years, I can compound that to 500,000 subscribers. And I reckon if I can get 500,000 subscribers, I might be able to generate a quarter of a million pounds, which was my kind of north star, which I decided if I can get that, I’ll be able to retire. I’ll be able to do nicer things. I won’t feel so trapped.
And this brings us on to our first lesson here: not at one point did I ever look at YouTube or any of this and go, “I’m scared this won’t pay me back for the time I put into it.” The real fear I had was, if I don’t do this, what will change?
And I see so many people—they want to start a YouTube channel, and they try it for a few videos and stop, and then they go back to their life that they’re not happy with, and they go back to chasing clients, and they go back to being someone who people ignore, and having to do all these things that they don’t want to do just to survive.
So, the first thing you have to realize is the real risk of not starting a YouTube channel is being in the same place in 10 years’ time. And that is the thing that scared me more than anything else.
Now, I’d made the call of going, “Right, I’m gonna go all in on this for 10 years.” I had another problem: I’ve got to run the business, I’ve got to make money, and I’ve got to try and grow a channel.
So, I got up at 5:00 a.m. and worked till 8:00 a.m., then did my job, then finished and did 6:00 till 10:00 at night YouTube, which is not sustainable. But again, I was driven by this fear that if I don’t do this, what’s going to happen to me?
And the way I looked at it was: if this really hurts for three years, or even ten, at least I’ve got thirty or forty years left where I’m going to be so comfortable I’ll always thank myself for those ten miserable years. And if you look at it in the grand scheme of your life, ten years of hard work—it’s not that much of a big deal.
So, I just kept hammering it, hammering it, hammering it. I was posting three videos a week and I was getting absolutely nowhere. And after about 18 months, I thought, “This is dumb. It doesn’t work.”
But I didn’t want to give in because I still had the looming disaster of the life I was still living. So, I changed strategy. I took it more seriously. I put more effort into the content, and it blew up the channel I used to have called Film Booth. And that is when everything changed.
Now, this is what people don’t understand about business or YouTube. We always assume the worst. We assume that the pain we’re in right now is going to remain forever even if we try. But even if you banged your head against a brick wall for ten years, eventually your head’s going to go through it, right?
So, what will happen to you is through being someone who can tolerate an immense amount of frustration, you will get ahead of everyone else who will eventually quit. And then what happens is you don’t need much before things start to change.
So, I have a video that blows up, and then the next one does well, then the next one does well. Then the biggest channels in the niche are emailing me. Then I’ve started to get invited onto podcasts.
So, if we look at the start of year two, I went from really struggling to the end—I was pretty much making more as a side hustle than my entire ten-year north star goal was. And then after that, what happens is we go full-time. And this is October 2021.
Then the turnover jumps up and up and up. And it doubled every year until we capped out at like 4 million this year. But keep in mind, I’m still not at the end of my ten years. I’ve got four years to go and I’m about fifty years ahead of where I ever thought I would be.
You will massively underestimate what is possible here. You’ll get tons of sponsorship requests. You’ll get people coming to you saying, “Do you want to help partner in my software with me?” You’ll get invited to do talks. You’ll get invited to do podcasts. You’ll get invited to invest in drink companies.
And then the final reason you kind of need to start today is: today is the easiest YouTube will ever be—ever again. Tomorrow, it’s going to be a little bit harder. Every day you don’t do this, you are basically shooting yourself in the foot.
And if you’re not very happy, and if you don’t see much future potential in your life, that’s on you.
And if you need any more inspiration, just watch this video here. It’s full of examples of businesses who have gone from very tough periods in their life to growing a channel, growing a business, making hundreds of thousands of dollars. And all of them have exactly the same pattern: they weren’t afraid to take action.
